========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hello, doctor. Well, I guess what brings me in is... I just don’t have the push I used to have. For my choir, or my quilting... I used to quilt all the time. My mama taught me on this heavy old Singer machine with the foot pedal. We used to use old flour sacks because they printed the prettiest little blue flowers on them back then, not like the plain bags you get at the store now. You could make a whole dress out of them if you had enough, or a nice blanket. 

But... oh, what was I saying? Right, I just don't have the drive for visiting folks or doing my sewing anymore. Most days I just sit at home. I keep the TV on real low... mostly just to have some noise, you know? I like watching the game shows, though the hosts talk awfully fast now. Not like Bob Barker. He always took his time. But I just sit there in my chair, even though I know people are expecting me at church and such. I just... I don't know, I just stay put.

Well, I suppose it's been a while now. It really started building up since I retired from the school office... that was a little over two years ago. I worked there for such a long time, right at the front desk. You wouldn't believe the things those middle schoolers would try to get away with. Always coming in saying they forgot their lunch money, but I knew half of them were just spending it on those sour candies at the corner store before the bell rang. I used to keep a little jar of peppermints on my desk, though. The real kind, the ones that melt in your mouth, not those hard ones that crack your teeth. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he always told me to watch out for the hard candies. He had this big fish tank in his waiting room, beautiful thing, full of those little neon tetras... but anyway, yes, it's been about two years since I left the school, and that's when things just started slowing down for me.

No, I don't think I ever did. I was always just so busy before, you know? I never really had the time to just sit around. Between the school and keeping up the house... I used to keep everything spotless. We had this old Hoover vacuum cleaner. Oh, it was heavy, heavier than a sack of potatoes. 

My husband bought it from one of those traveling salesmen. You don't see them much anymore, do you? Men coming right up to your front porch with a suitcase full of brushes or vacuums. He was wearing a very sharp suit, I remember that clearly. A nice grey wool suit, even though it was the middle of July and hot as an oven outside. He must have been sweating up a storm under all that wool. I felt so bad for him I offered him a glass of lemonade... I used to make it from scratch with the lemons from my sister's tree. She had this beautiful Meyer lemon tree in her backyard. They make the sweetest lemonade, you don't even need to add much sugar. 

But... oh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling low like this. No, I never really felt this way before I retired. I was always on the go.

Always coming in saying they forgot their lunch money, but I knew half of them were just spending it on those sour candies at the corner store before the bell rang. I used to keep a little jar of peppermints on my desk, though. The real kind, the ones that melt in your mouth, not those hard ones that crack your teeth. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he always told me to watch out for the hard candies. He had this big fish tank in his waiting room, beautiful thing, full of those little neon tetras... but anyway, yes, it's been about two years since I left the school, and that's when things just started slowing down for me.\n\nDoctor: Before this two-year period started, have you ever had a time in your life—maybe weeks or months—when you felt this same kind of low mood, lack of energy, or loss of interest in things you normally enjoyed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I ever did. I was always just so busy before, you know? I never really had the time to just sit around. Between the school and keeping up the house... I used to keep everything spotless. We had this old Hoover vacuum cleaner. Oh, it was heavy, heavier than a sack of potatoes. \n\nMy husband bought it from one of those traveling salesmen. You don't see them much anymore, do you? Men coming right up to your front porch with a suitcase full of brushes or vacuums. He was wearing a very sharp suit, I remember that clearly. A nice grey wool suit, even though it was the middle of July and hot as an oven outside. He must have been sweating up a storm under all that wool. I felt so bad for him I offered him a glass of lemonade... I used to make it from scratch with the lemons from my sister's tree. She had this beautiful Meyer lemon tree in her backyard. They make the sweetest lemonade, you don't even need to add much sugar. \n\nBut... oh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling low like this. No, I never really felt this way before I retired. I was always on the go.\n\n[Last Question]\nGoing back further—to when you were younger, in your twenties or thirties, or even in your forties—did you ever go through a period where you felt sad, down, or depressed, even if it didn't last as long as what you're experiencing now?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, let me think back... my twenties and thirties. Gosh, that feels like a lifetime ago, doesn't it? I was married at twenty-two. My husband, he worked down at the rail yard. He used to come home smelling like diesel and grease every single day. I had to scrub his work shirts with a special lye soap just to get the stains out. My mother made that soap herself, you know. She had a big iron kettle she'd set up in the backyard over a wood fire. She'd use the ashes from the fireplace and leftover bacon grease. It smelled awful while it was cooking, just terrible, but it cleaned better than anything you can buy in a plastic bottle at the Piggly Wiggly today. 

We used to shop at the Piggly Wiggly every Saturday morning. They had a butcher there, Mr. Henderson. He always saved the best cuts of roast for me because I'd bring him a slice of my pecan pie. I used my grandmother's recipe for the crust, made with real lard, not that shortening stuff... 

But, what was your question? Oh, being sad back then. No, I don't recall ever being depressed when I was younger. I was just busy raising the children and keeping the house together. I didn't have time to be sad.

I was always just so busy before, you know? I never really had the time to just sit around. Between the school and keeping up the house... I used to keep everything spotless. We had this old Hoover vacuum cleaner. Oh, it was heavy, heavier than a sack of potatoes. \n\nMy husband bought it from one of those traveling salesmen. You don't see them much anymore, do you? I felt so bad for him I offered him a glass of lemonade... I used to make it from scratch with the lemons from my sister's tree. She had this beautiful Meyer lemon tree in her backyard. Gosh, that feels like a lifetime ago, doesn't it? I was married at twenty-two. My husband, he worked down at the rail yard. He used to come home smelling like diesel and grease every single day. I had to scrub his work shirts with a special lye soap just to get the stains out. My mother made that soap herself, you know. She had a big iron kettle she'd set up in the backyard over a wood fire. She'd use the ashes from the fireplace and leftover bacon grease. It smelled awful while it was cooking, just terrible, but it cleaned better than anything you can buy in a plastic bottle at the Piggly Wiggly today. \n\nWe used to shop at the Piggly Wiggly every Saturday morning. They had a butcher there, Mr. Henderson. He always saved the best cuts of roast for me because I'd bring him a slice of my pecan pie. I used my grandmother's recipe for the crust, made with real lard, not that shortening stuff... \n\nBut, what was your question? Oh, being sad back then. No, I don't recall ever being depressed when I was younger. I was just busy raising the children and keeping the house together. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, my memory... I don't think it's any worse than anyone else my age. I mean, I might misplace my reading glasses now and then. I bought these new ones at the drugstore, the ones with the little rhinestones on the corners. They look just like a pair my aunt used to wear. She was a very fashionable lady, my aunt. She used to take the train up to Chicago twice a year just to buy hats. Real hats, with feathers and netting, not those baseball caps everyone wears nowadays. She had this one hatbox, it was covered in velvet... a deep, rich burgundy color. She kept her favorite Sunday hat in there. 

I remember the train station where she'd leave from, it had these huge brass clocks hanging from the ceiling. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. They tore that station down back in the eighties to build a parking garage. Such a shame. They poured all that concrete, and it's always so dark and damp in there. My neighbor parked there once and someone stole the hubcaps right off his Buick... 

But my memory, no, I don't think I'm forgetting conversations or anything like that. Just the normal things, like where I put my glasses.

Trouble with bills? Oh, no, I always pay them the very day they come in the mail. I sit right down at the kitchen table with my checkbook. I don't trust doing it on the computer like the young folks do. You have to buy stamps for the envelopes, though, and they just keep raising the price of stamps! It's a crying shame. I remember when a stamp had a beautiful picture of a locomotive on it, back when they were just a few pennies. 

My brother loved trains. He had a whole model train set down in our basement, took up two whole ping-pong tables. He painted all the little trees and houses by hand with a tiny little brush... he got the brushes from a hobby shop downtown. The man who ran that shop had a glass eye. It didn't move when he looked at you, just stared straight ahead. Used to give me the shivers when I was a girl. He lost his real eye in the war, my mother said. 

But... what did you ask me about? Oh, appointments and medicines. No, I just write everything down on the calendar on the fridge. The one with the little kittens on it. So I don't have any trouble keeping track.

younger, in your twenties or thirties, or even in your forties—did you ever go through a period where you felt sad, down, or depressed, even if it didn't last as long as what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think back... my twenties and thirties. Gosh, that feels like a lifetime ago, doesn't it? I was married at twenty-two. My husband, he worked down at the rail yard. He used to come home smelling like diesel and grease every single day. I had to scrub his work shirts with a special lye soap just to get the stains out. My mother made that soap herself, you know. She had a big iron kettle she'd set up in the backyard over a wood fire. She'd use the ashes from the fireplace and leftover bacon grease. It smelled awful while it was cooking, just terrible, but it cleaned better than anything you can buy in a plastic bottle at the Piggly Wiggly today. \n\nWe used to shop at the Piggly Wiggly every Saturday morning. They had a butcher there, Mr. Henderson. He always saved the best cuts of roast for me because I'd bring him a slice of my pecan pie. I used my grandmother's recipe for the crust, made with real lard, not that shortening stuff... \n\nBut, what was your question? Oh, being sad back then. No, I don't recall ever being depressed when I was younger. I was just busy raising the children and keeping the house together. I didn't have time to be sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory—like forgetting things more often than you used to, or having trouble remembering recent conversations or events?\n\nPatient: Well, my memory... I don't think it's any worse than anyone else my age. I mean, I might misplace my reading glasses now and then. I bought these new ones at the drugstore, the ones with the little rhinestones on the corners. They look just like a pair my aunt used to wear. She was a very fashionable lady, my aunt. She used to take the train up to Chicago twice a year just to buy hats. Real hats, with feathers and netting, not those baseball caps everyone wears nowadays. She had this one hatbox, it was covered in velvet... a deep, rich burgundy color. She kept her favorite Sunday hat in there. \n\nI remember the train station where she'd leave from, it had these huge brass clocks hanging from the ceiling. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. They tore that station down back in the eighties to build a parking garage. Such a shame. They poured all that concrete, and it's always so dark and damp in there. My neighbor parked there once and someone stole the hubcaps right off his Buick... \n\nBut my memory, no, I don't think I'm forgetting conversations or anything like that. Just the normal things, like where I put my glasses.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble paying your bills on time, managing your medications, or keeping track of appointments—things you used to handle without any problem?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? Oh, no, I always pay them the very day they come in the mail. I sit right down at the kitchen table with my checkbook. I don't trust doing it on the computer like the young folks do. You have to buy stamps for the envelopes, though, and they just keep raising the price of stamps! It's a crying shame. I remember when a stamp had a beautiful picture of a locomotive on it, back when they were just a few pennies. \n\nMy brother loved trains. He had a whole model train set down in our basement, took up two whole ping-pong tables. He painted all the little trees and houses by hand with a tiny little brush... he got the brushes from a hobby shop downtown. The man who ran that shop had a glass eye. It didn't move when he looked at you, just stared straight ahead. Used to give me the shivers when I was a girl. He lost his real eye in the war, my mother said. \n\nBut... what did you ask me about? Oh, appointments and medicines. No, I just write everything down on the calendar on the fridge. The one with the little kittens on it. So I don't have any trouble keeping track.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me what year it is right now, and who is the current president of the United States?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, the year... let me think a second. It's 2023, isn't it? And the president... that's Mr. Biden. I saw him on the television the other day when I was flipping through the channels trying to find my stories. I don't watch the news much anymore, it's always so depressing. Just bad news all the time. 

My television set is getting quite old, actually. The picture gets a bit fuzzy sometimes, especially when it rains. It's one of those heavy ones, not the flat ones they stick right on the wall like a picture frame. My son-in-law offered to buy me one of those flat ones for Christmas last year, but I told him no thank you. I don't trust them not to fall right off the wall and smash into a million pieces on the carpet. 

I have a beautiful Persian rug in the living room, you see. My late husband bought it for our tenth anniversary. We went to this little shop downtown, run by a man named Mr. Yilmaz. He served us hot tea in these tiny little glasses with gold rims while we looked at the rugs. It was so strong, the tea, and he put three cubes of sugar in mine. I don't usually take sugar in my tea, I prefer just a slice of lemon. My grandmother had a lemon tree in her sunroom... 

Wait, did I answer your question? Yes, 2023 and President Biden.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yesterday... well, I got up and had a bowl of oatmeal. I usually have the oatmeal from that round cardboard tube, you know, the one with the man in the old-fashioned hat on the front. I always wonder why they never update his picture. Though I suppose hats like that might come back into style. I saw a young girl walking past my window the other day wearing a bright yellow bucket hat. It looked exactly like the little hats we used to put on the babies to keep the sun off their noses. 

You have to be so careful with the sun. I fell asleep at the beach once when I was a teenager, down in Florida, and I turned as red as a boiled lobster. My skin was just radiating heat! I had to soak in a bathtub full of cold tea just to take the sting out. The bathtub in that motel was one of those heavy clawfoot ones, real cast iron. They hold the cold so well, but they are an absolute nightmare to clean. You have to get right down on your hands and knees with a little scrub brush to get the dust out from around the feet. My knees can't take that kind of scrubbing anymore, they just pop and ache if I kneel on the linoleum too long... 

Wait, what did you ask me? Oh, lunch. I don't really remember what I had for lunch, to be honest. Maybe just a piece of toast. I didn't do much yesterday, just stayed around the house.

Children? Well, no, I don't have any children. Never had any babies of my own. My husband and I, we just had our little dog, Buster. He was a beagle, always had his nose right to the ground, sniffing out everything. If you dropped even a single crumb of a cracker, he’d find it in a second.

People used to save them to store their sewing supplies. My Aunt Betty kept all her spare buttons in an old blue cookie tin. Hundreds of buttons, all different colors and sizes, made of glass and wood and plastic. I used to string them on a piece of heavy thread to make little necklaces when I was a girl. 

Thread was much stronger back then, I think. You could really pull on it and it wouldn't snap. My mother used to mend all our clothes on this heavy black Singer sewing machine that you had to pump with your foot. The pedal was made of iron and it had this beautiful, intricate pattern cast right into it, like vines or something. It squeaked something awful if you didn't oil it regularly, though. She kept a little metal can of oil with a long spout right next to the spools... 

Wait, what were we talking about? Oh, children. No, I never had any. Just Buster.

Most days I just sit at home. I keep the TV on real low... mostly just to have some noise, you know? I like watching the game shows, though the hosts talk awfully fast now. Not like Bob Barker. He always took his time. But I just sit there in my chair, even though I know people are expecting me at church and such. I just... I don't know, I just stay put.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this lack of drive and just wanting to sit at home? Has it been going on for a few weeks, or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's been a while now. It really started building up since I retired from the school office... that was a little over two years ago. I worked there for such a long time, right at the front desk. You wouldn't believe the things those middle schoolers would try to get away with. Always coming in saying they forgot their lunch money, but I knew half of them were just spending it on those sour candies at the corner store before the bell rang. I used to keep a little jar of peppermints on my desk, though. The real kind, the ones that melt in your mouth, not those hard ones that crack your teeth. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he always told me to watch out for the hard candies. He had this big fish tank in his waiting room, beautiful thing, full of those little neon tetras... but anyway, yes, it's been about two years since I left the school, and that's when things just started slowing down for me.\n\nDoctor: Before this two-year period started, have you ever had a time in your life—maybe weeks or months—when you felt this same kind of low mood, lack of energy, or loss of interest in things you normally enjoyed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I ever did. I was always just so busy before, you know? I never really had the time to just sit around. Between the school and keeping up the house... I used to keep everything spotless. We had this old Hoover vacuum cleaner. Oh, it was heavy, heavier than a sack of potatoes. \n\nMy husband bought it from one of those traveling salesmen. You don't see them much anymore, do you? Men coming right up to your front porch with a suitcase full of brushes or vacuums. He was wearing a very sharp suit, I remember that clearly. A nice grey wool suit, even though it was the middle of July and hot as an oven outside. He must have been sweating up a storm under all that wool. I felt so bad for him I offered him a glass of lemonade... I used to make it from scratch with the lemons from my sister's tree. She had this beautiful Meyer lemon tree in her backyard. They make the sweetest lemonade, you don't even need to add much sugar. \n\nBut... oh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling low like this. No, I never really felt this way before I retired. I was always on the go.\n\nDoctor: Going back further—to when you were younger, in your twenties or thirties, or even in your forties—did you ever go through a period where you felt sad, down, or depressed, even if it didn't last as long as what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think back... my twenties and thirties. Gosh, that feels like a lifetime ago, doesn't it? I was married at twenty-two. My husband, he worked down at the rail yard. He used to come home smelling like diesel and grease every single day. I had to scrub his work shirts with a special lye soap just to get the stains out. My mother made that soap herself, you know. She had a big iron kettle she'd set up in the backyard over a wood fire. She'd use the ashes from the fireplace and leftover bacon grease. It smelled awful while it was cooking, just terrible, but it cleaned better than anything you can buy in a plastic bottle at the Piggly Wiggly today. \n\nWe used to shop at the Piggly Wiggly every Saturday morning. They had a butcher there, Mr. Henderson. He always saved the best cuts of roast for me because I'd bring him a slice of my pecan pie. I used my grandmother's recipe for the crust, made with real lard, not that shortening stuff... \n\nBut, what was your question? Oh, being sad back then. No, I don't recall ever being depressed when I was younger. I was just busy raising the children and keeping the house together. I didn't have time to be sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory—like forgetting things more often than you used to, or having trouble remembering recent conversations or events?\n\nPatient: Well, my memory... I don't think it's any worse than anyone else my age. I mean, I might misplace my reading glasses now and then. I bought these new ones at the drugstore, the ones with the little rhinestones on the corners. They look just like a pair my aunt used to wear. She was a very fashionable lady, my aunt. She used to take the train up to Chicago twice a year just to buy hats. Real hats, with feathers and netting, not those baseball caps everyone wears nowadays. She had this one hatbox, it was covered in velvet... a deep, rich burgundy color. She kept her favorite Sunday hat in there. \n\nI remember the train station where she'd leave from, it had these huge brass clocks hanging from the ceiling. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. They tore that station down back in the eighties to build a parking garage. Such a shame. They poured all that concrete, and it's always so dark and damp in there. My neighbor parked there once and someone stole the hubcaps right off his Buick... \n\nBut my memory, no, I don't think I'm forgetting conversations or anything like that. Just the normal things, like where I put my glasses.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble paying your bills on time, managing your medications, or keeping track of appointments—things you used to handle without any problem?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? Oh, no, I always pay them the very day they come in the mail. I sit right down at the kitchen table with my checkbook. I don't trust doing it on the computer like the young folks do. You have to buy stamps for the envelopes, though, and they just keep raising the price of stamps! It's a crying shame. I remember when a stamp had a beautiful picture of a locomotive on it, back when they were just a few pennies. \n\nMy brother loved trains. He had a whole model train set down in our basement, took up two whole ping-pong tables. He painted all the little trees and houses by hand with a tiny little brush... he got the brushes from a hobby shop downtown. The man who ran that shop had a glass eye. It didn't move when he looked at you, just stared straight ahead. Used to give me the shivers when I was a girl. He lost his real eye in the war, my mother said. \n\nBut... what did you ask me about? Oh, appointments and medicines. No, I just write everything down on the calendar on the fridge. The one with the little kittens on it. So I don't have any trouble keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what year it is right now, and who is the current president of the United States?\n\nPatient: Well, the year... let me think a second. It's 2023, isn't it? And the president... that's Mr. Biden. I saw him on the television the other day when I was flipping through the channels trying to find my stories. I don't watch the news much anymore, it's always so depressing. Just bad news all the time. \n\nMy television set is getting quite old, actually. The picture gets a bit fuzzy sometimes, especially when it rains. It's one of those heavy ones, not the flat ones they stick right on the wall like a picture frame. My son-in-law offered to buy me one of those flat ones for Christmas last year, but I told him no thank you. I don't trust them not to fall right off the wall and smash into a million pieces on the carpet. \n\nI have a beautiful Persian rug in the living room, you see. My late husband bought it for our tenth anniversary. We went to this little shop downtown, run by a man named Mr. Yilmaz. He served us hot tea in these tiny little glasses with gold rims while we looked at the rugs. It was so strong, the tea, and he put three cubes of sugar in mine. I don't usually take sugar in my tea, I prefer just a slice of lemon. My grandmother had a lemon tree in her sunroom... \n\nWait, did I answer your question? Yes, 2023 and President Biden.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your day yesterday? What did you do, and do you remember what you had for breakfast or lunch?\n\nPatient: Yesterday... well, I got up and had a bowl of oatmeal. I usually have the oatmeal from that round cardboard tube, you know, the one with the man in the old-fashioned hat on the front. I always wonder why they never update his picture. Though I suppose hats like that might come back into style. I saw a young girl walking past my window the other day wearing a bright yellow bucket hat. It looked exactly like the little hats we used to put on the babies to keep the sun off their noses. \n\nYou have to be so careful with the sun. I fell asleep at the beach once when I was a teenager, down in Florida, and I turned as red as a boiled lobster. My skin was just radiating heat! I had to soak in a bathtub full of cold tea just to take the sting out. The bathtub in that motel was one of those heavy clawfoot ones, real cast iron. They hold the cold so well, but they are an absolute nightmare to clean. You have to get right down on your hands and knees with a little scrub brush to get the dust out from around the feet. My knees can't take that kind of scrubbing anymore, they just pop and ache if I kneel on the linoleum too long... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, lunch. I don't really remember what I had for lunch, to be honest. Maybe just a piece of toast. I didn't do much yesterday, just stayed around the house.\n\nDoctor: I'd like you to think carefully: Can you tell me the names of your children or grandchildren, and do you see them or talk to them regularly?\n\nPatient: Children? Well, no, I don't have any children. Never had any babies of my own. My husband and I, we just had our little dog, Buster. He was a beagle, always had his nose right to the ground, sniffing out everything. If you dropped even a single crumb of a cracker, he’d find it in a second. \n\nWe used to buy those saltine crackers in the big metal tins, the ones with the tight lids. You really don't see those tins much anymore at the grocery store. People used to save them to store their sewing supplies. My Aunt Betty kept all her spare buttons in an old blue cookie tin. Hundreds of buttons, all different colors and sizes, made of glass and wood and plastic. I used to string them on a piece of heavy thread to make little necklaces when I was a girl. \n\nThread was much stronger back then, I think. You could really pull on it and it wouldn't snap. My mother used to mend all our clothes on this heavy black Singer sewing machine that you had to pump with your foot. The pedal was made of iron and it had this beautiful, intricate pattern cast right into it, like vines or something. It squeaked something awful if you didn't oil it regularly, though. She kept a little metal can of oil with a long spout right next to the spools... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, children. No, I never had any. Just Buster.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
```json
{"candidates": ["F03.90", "F32.9"], "note": "Patient demonstrates significant cognitive changes: tangential speech with excessive detail and difficulty maintaining conversational thread, confabulation (claims never had children but previously mentioned 'raising the children'), disorientation to personal history, and inability to recall recent events (lunch yesterday). These cognitive findings support neurocognitive disorder. However, depression remains in differential given 2-year depressive episode onset after retirement with anhedonia and avolition. Need to clarify cognitive vs. mood primacy.", "is_final": false}
```

I didn't do much yesterday, just stayed around the house.\n\nDoctor: I'd like you to think carefully: Can you tell me the names of your children or grandchildren, and do you see them or talk to them regularly?\n\nPatient: Children? He was a beagle, always had his nose right to the ground, sniffing out everything. If you dropped even a single crumb of a cracker, he’d find it in a second. \n\nWe used to buy those saltine crackers in the big metal tins, the ones with the tight lids. You really don't see those tins much anymore at the grocery store. People used to save them to store their sewing supplies. My Aunt Betty kept all her spare buttons in an old blue cookie tin. Hundreds of buttons, all different colors and sizes, made of glass and wood and plastic. I used to string them on a piece of heavy thread to make little necklaces when I was a girl. \n\nThread was much stronger back then, I think. You could really pull on it and it wouldn't snap. My mother used to mend all our clothes on this heavy black Singer sewing machine that you had to pump with your foot. The pedal was made of iron and it had this beautiful, intricate pattern cast right into it, like vines or something. It squeaked something awful if you didn't oil it regularly, though. She kept a little metal can of oil with a long spout right next to the spools... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, children. No, I never had any. Just Buster.\n\n[Last Question]\nYou mentioned you never had children, but earlier you said you retired from working at a school office and that you used to keep a jar of peppermints on your desk. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
The school office... yes, I worked there for a long time. I was the one who answered the phones and made sure the attendance sheets were collected. The teachers would send these little slips of paper down to the office in these heavy plastic folders. They were a sort of mustard yellow color, those folders, with a little metal clasp that was always getting bent out of shape. 

I tried to fix one once with a pair of pliers, but I just ended up pinching my finger. It blistered right up. I had to keep it wrapped in a bandage for a week, and it made typing on the typewriter nearly impossible. We had one of those big, heavy IBM Selectrics, the ones with the little metal ball that spun around and smacked the paper. It made such a satisfying clicking noise. 

You don't hear noises like that anymore with these computers. Everything is just tapping on little plastic squares. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she got a computer to send emails to her sister in Ohio. She said it took her three weeks just to figure out how to turn the thing on. I told her she ought to just write a letter. People don't write letters anymore. I used to have a beautiful set of stationery, pale blue with little white daisies embossed in the corner. I bought it at a stationery shop that used to be right next to the old movie theater downtown. They tore that theater down years ago to put up a parking garage. It was a shame, really, they had these beautiful velvet curtains in the lobby... 

Wait, did you ask me how long I worked there? Oh, I don't really remember the exact number of years. It was a good long while, though. I just did the phones and the attendance.

I felt so bad for him I offered him a glass of lemonade... I used to make it from scratch with the lemons from my sister's tree. She had this beautiful Meyer lemon tree in her backyard. Gosh, that feels like a lifetime ago, doesn't it? I was married at twenty-two. My husband, he worked down at the rail yard. He used to come home smelling like diesel and grease every single day. She used to take the train up to Chicago twice a year just to buy hats. Real hats, with feathers and netting, not those baseball caps everyone wears nowadays. She had this one hatbox, it was covered in velvet... a deep, rich burgundy color.   "candidates": ["F03.90", "F32.9", "G30.9"],
  "note": "Added G30.9 (Alzheimer's disease, unspecified). Patient demonstrates significant cognitive concerns: tangential speech with excessive detail and difficulty maintaining thought (repeatedly losing track of questions), inability to recall number of years worked despite claiming 'a long time', inconsistency about having children (initially said 'no children' then mentioned 'raising the children'), difficulty recalling lunch yesterday, and marked difficulty with executive function. I was always just so busy before, you know? I never really had the time to just sit around. Between the school and keeping up the house... I used to keep everything spotless. We had this old Hoover vacuum cleaner. Oh, it was heavy, heavier than a sack of potatoes. \n\nMy husband bought it from one of those traveling salesmen. You don't see them much anymore, do you? Men coming right up to your front porch with a suitcase full of brushes or vacuums. He was wearing a very sharp suit, I remember that clearly. A nice grey wool suit, even though it was the middle of July and hot as an oven outside. He must have been sweating up a storm under all that wool. I felt so bad for him I offered him a glass of lemonade... I used to make it from scratch with the lemons from my sister's tree. She had this beautiful Meyer lemon tree in her backyard. They make the sweetest lemonade, you don't even need to add much sugar. \n\nBut... oh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling low like this. No, I never really felt this way before I retired. I was always on the go.\n\nDoctor: Going back further—to when you were younger, in your twenties or thirties, or even in your forties—did you ever go through a period where you felt sad, down, or depressed, even if it didn't last as long as what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think back... my twenties and thirties. Gosh, that feels like a lifetime ago, doesn't it? I was married at twenty-two. My husband, he worked down at the rail yard. He used to come home smelling like diesel and grease every single day. I had to scrub his work shirts with a special lye soap just to get the stains out. My mother made that soap herself, you know. She had a big iron kettle she'd set up in the backyard over a wood fire. She'd use the ashes from the fireplace and leftover bacon grease. It smelled awful while it was cooking, just terrible, but it cleaned better than anything you can buy in a plastic bottle at the Piggly Wiggly today. \n\nWe used to shop at the Piggly Wiggly every Saturday morning. They had a butcher there, Mr. Henderson. He always saved the best cuts of roast for me because I'd bring him a slice of my pecan pie. I used my grandmother's recipe for the crust, made with real lard, not that shortening stuff... \n\nBut, what was your question? Oh, being sad back then. No, I don't recall ever being depressed when I was younger. I was just busy raising the children and keeping the house together. I didn't have time to be sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory—like forgetting things more often than you used to, or having trouble remembering recent conversations or events?\n\nPatient: Well, my memory... I don't think it's any worse than anyone else my age. I mean, I might misplace my reading glasses now and then. I bought these new ones at the drugstore, the ones with the little rhinestones on the corners. They look just like a pair my aunt used to wear. She was a very fashionable lady, my aunt. She used to take the train up to Chicago twice a year just to buy hats. Real hats, with feathers and netting, not those baseball caps everyone wears nowadays. She had this one hatbox, it was covered in velvet... a deep, rich burgundy color. She kept her favorite Sunday hat in there. \n\nI remember the train station where she'd leave from, it had these huge brass clocks hanging from the ceiling. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. They tore that station down back in the eighties to build a parking garage. Such a shame. They poured all that concrete, and it's always so dark and damp in there. My neighbor parked there once and someone stole the hubcaps right off his Buick... \n\nBut my memory, no, I don't think I'm forgetting conversations or anything like that. Just the normal things, like where I put my glasses.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble paying your bills on time, managing your medications, or keeping track of appointments—things you used to handle without any problem?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? Oh, no, I always pay them the very day they come in the mail. I sit right down at the kitchen table with my checkbook. I don't trust doing it on the computer like the young folks do. You have to buy stamps for the envelopes, though, and they just keep raising the price of stamps! It's a crying shame. I remember when a stamp had a beautiful picture of a locomotive on it, back when they were just a few pennies. \n\nMy brother loved trains. He had a whole model train set down in our basement, took up two whole ping-pong tables. He painted all the little trees and houses by hand with a tiny little brush... he got the brushes from a hobby shop downtown. The man who ran that shop had a glass eye. It didn't move when he looked at you, just stared straight ahead. Used to give me the shivers when I was a girl. He lost his real eye in the war, my mother said. \n\nBut... what did you ask me about? Oh, appointments and medicines. No, I just write everything down on the calendar on the fridge. The one with the little kittens on it. So I don't have any trouble keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what year it is right now, and who is the current president of the United States?\n\nPatient: Well, the year... let me think a second. It's 2023, isn't it? And the president... that's Mr. Biden. I saw him on the television the other day when I was flipping through the channels trying to find my stories. I don't watch the news much anymore, it's always so depressing. Just bad news all the time. \n\nMy television set is getting quite old, actually. The picture gets a bit fuzzy sometimes, especially when it rains. It's one of those heavy ones, not the flat ones they stick right on the wall like a picture frame. My son-in-law offered to buy me one of those flat ones for Christmas last year, but I told him no thank you. I don't trust them not to fall right off the wall and smash into a million pieces on the carpet. \n\nI have a beautiful Persian rug in the living room, you see. My late husband bought it for our tenth anniversary. We went to this little shop downtown, run by a man named Mr. Yilmaz. He served us hot tea in these tiny little glasses with gold rims while we looked at the rugs. It was so strong, the tea, and he put three cubes of sugar in mine. I don't usually take sugar in my tea, I prefer just a slice of lemon. My grandmother had a lemon tree in her sunroom... \n\nWait, did I answer your question? Yes, 2023 and President Biden.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your day yesterday? What did you do, and do you remember what you had for breakfast or lunch?\n\nPatient: Yesterday... well, I got up and had a bowl of oatmeal. I usually have the oatmeal from that round cardboard tube, you know, the one with the man in the old-fashioned hat on the front. I always wonder why they never update his picture. Though I suppose hats like that might come back into style. I saw a young girl walking past my window the other day wearing a bright yellow bucket hat. It looked exactly like the little hats we used to put on the babies to keep the sun off their noses. \n\nYou have to be so careful with the sun. I fell asleep at the beach once when I was a teenager, down in Florida, and I turned as red as a boiled lobster. My skin was just radiating heat! I had to soak in a bathtub full of cold tea just to take the sting out. The bathtub in that motel was one of those heavy clawfoot ones, real cast iron. They hold the cold so well, but they are an absolute nightmare to clean. You have to get right down on your hands and knees with a little scrub brush to get the dust out from around the feet. My knees can't take that kind of scrubbing anymore, they just pop and ache if I kneel on the linoleum too long... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, lunch. I don't really remember what I had for lunch, to be honest. Maybe just a piece of toast. I didn't do much yesterday, just stayed around the house.\n\nDoctor: I'd like you to think carefully: Can you tell me the names of your children or grandchildren, and do you see them or talk to them regularly?\n\nPatient: Children? Well, no, I don't have any children. Never had any babies of my own. My husband and I, we just had our little dog, Buster. He was a beagle, always had his nose right to the ground, sniffing out everything. If you dropped even a single crumb of a cracker, he’d find it in a second. \n\nWe used to buy those saltine crackers in the big metal tins, the ones with the tight lids. You really don't see those tins much anymore at the grocery store. People used to save them to store their sewing supplies. My Aunt Betty kept all her spare buttons in an old blue cookie tin. Hundreds of buttons, all different colors and sizes, made of glass and wood and plastic. I used to string them on a piece of heavy thread to make little necklaces when I was a girl. \n\nThread was much stronger back then, I think. You could really pull on it and it wouldn't snap. My mother used to mend all our clothes on this heavy black Singer sewing machine that you had to pump with your foot. The pedal was made of iron and it had this beautiful, intricate pattern cast right into it, like vines or something. It squeaked something awful if you didn't oil it regularly, though. She kept a little metal can of oil with a long spout right next to the spools... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, children. No, I never had any. Just Buster.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you never had children, but earlier you said you retired from working at a school office and that you used to keep a jar of peppermints on your desk. Can you tell me more about what you did at that school office—what were your main duties, and how many years did you work there?\n\nPatient: The school office... yes, I worked there for a long time. I was the one who answered the phones and made sure the attendance sheets were collected. The teachers would send these little slips of paper down to the office in these heavy plastic folders. They were a sort of mustard yellow color, those folders, with a little metal clasp that was always getting bent out of shape. \n\nI tried to fix one once with a pair of pliers, but I just ended up pinching my finger. It blistered right up. I had to keep it wrapped in a bandage for a week, and it made typing on the typewriter nearly impossible. We had one of those big, heavy IBM Selectrics, the ones with the little metal ball that spun around and smacked the paper. It made such a satisfying clicking noise. \n\nYou don't hear noises like that anymore with these computers. Everything is just tapping on little plastic squares. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she got a computer to send emails to her sister in Ohio. She said it took her three weeks just to figure out how to turn the thing on. I told her she ought to just write a letter. People don't write letters anymore. I used to have a beautiful set of stationery, pale blue with little white daisies embossed in the corner. I bought it at a stationery shop that used to be right next to the old movie theater downtown. They tore that theater down years ago to put up a parking garage. It was a shame, really, they had these beautiful velvet curtains in the lobby... \n\nWait, did you ask me how long I worked there? Oh, I don't really remember the exact number of years. It was a good long while, though. I just did the phones and the attendance.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Christmas? Well... I don't really know what I got. I suppose I didn't get much of anything, maybe just a new pair of slippers. I always liked those soft ones with the little bit of fleece on the inside. They keep your toes so warm in the mornings, especially when the floorboards are drafty.

Dogs have incredible hearing. I read once that they can hear a whistle that a human can't even pick up. It makes you wonder what else they're hearing that we don't. 

Like the birds, maybe. The crows have been so loud lately out back. They gather up in that big elm tree and just squawk at each other all morning. I tried throwing a little piece of bread out for them, but they just looked at it and kept yelling. Crows are very smart, you know. They can remember faces. If you do something mean to a crow, it will remember you forever and tell all its friends. I saw a program about it on the television...

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF03.90, F32.9, G30.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, I guess what brings me in is... I just don’t have the push I used to have. For my choir, or my quilting... I used to quilt all the time. My mama taught me on this heavy old Singer machine with the foot pedal. We used to use old flour sacks because they printed the prettiest little blue flowers on them back then, not like the plain bags you get at the store now. You could make a whole dress out of them if you had enough, or a nice blanket. \n\nBut... oh, what was I saying? Right, I just don't have the drive for visiting folks or doing my sewing anymore. Most days I just sit at home. I keep the TV on real low... mostly just to have some noise, you know? I like watching the game shows, though the hosts talk awfully fast now. Not like Bob Barker. He always took his time. But I just sit there in my chair, even though I know people are expecting me at church and such. I just... I don't know, I just stay put.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this lack of drive and just wanting to sit at home? Has it been going on for a few weeks, or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's been a while now. It really started building up since I retired from the school office... that was a little over two years ago. I worked there for such a long time, right at the front desk. You wouldn't believe the things those middle schoolers would try to get away with. Always coming in saying they forgot their lunch money, but I knew half of them were just spending it on those sour candies at the corner store before the bell rang. I used to keep a little jar of peppermints on my desk, though. The real kind, the ones that melt in your mouth, not those hard ones that crack your teeth. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he always told me to watch out for the hard candies. He had this big fish tank in his waiting room, beautiful thing, full of those little neon tetras... but anyway, yes, it's been about two years since I left the school, and that's when things just started slowing down for me.\n\nDoctor: Before this two-year period started, have you ever had a time in your life—maybe weeks or months—when you felt this same kind of low mood, lack of energy, or loss of interest in things you normally enjoyed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I ever did. I was always just so busy before, you know? I never really had the time to just sit around. Between the school and keeping up the house... I used to keep everything spotless. We had this old Hoover vacuum cleaner. Oh, it was heavy, heavier than a sack of potatoes. \n\nMy husband bought it from one of those traveling salesmen. You don't see them much anymore, do you? Men coming right up to your front porch with a suitcase full of brushes or vacuums. He was wearing a very sharp suit, I remember that clearly. A nice grey wool suit, even though it was the middle of July and hot as an oven outside. He must have been sweating up a storm under all that wool. I felt so bad for him I offered him a glass of lemonade... I used to make it from scratch with the lemons from my sister's tree. She had this beautiful Meyer lemon tree in her backyard. They make the sweetest lemonade, you don't even need to add much sugar. \n\nBut... oh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling low like this. No, I never really felt this way before I retired. I was always on the go.\n\nDoctor: Going back further—to when you were younger, in your twenties or thirties, or even in your forties—did you ever go through a period where you felt sad, down, or depressed, even if it didn't last as long as what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think back... my twenties and thirties. Gosh, that feels like a lifetime ago, doesn't it? I was married at twenty-two. My husband, he worked down at the rail yard. He used to come home smelling like diesel and grease every single day. I had to scrub his work shirts with a special lye soap just to get the stains out. My mother made that soap herself, you know. She had a big iron kettle she'd set up in the backyard over a wood fire. She'd use the ashes from the fireplace and leftover bacon grease. It smelled awful while it was cooking, just terrible, but it cleaned better than anything you can buy in a plastic bottle at the Piggly Wiggly today. \n\nWe used to shop at the Piggly Wiggly every Saturday morning. They had a butcher there, Mr. Henderson. He always saved the best cuts of roast for me because I'd bring him a slice of my pecan pie. I used my grandmother's recipe for the crust, made with real lard, not that shortening stuff... \n\nBut, what was your question? Oh, being sad back then. No, I don't recall ever being depressed when I was younger. I was just busy raising the children and keeping the house together. I didn't have time to be sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory—like forgetting things more often than you used to, or having trouble remembering recent conversations or events?\n\nPatient: Well, my memory... I don't think it's any worse than anyone else my age. I mean, I might misplace my reading glasses now and then. I bought these new ones at the drugstore, the ones with the little rhinestones on the corners. They look just like a pair my aunt used to wear. She was a very fashionable lady, my aunt. She used to take the train up to Chicago twice a year just to buy hats. Real hats, with feathers and netting, not those baseball caps everyone wears nowadays. She had this one hatbox, it was covered in velvet... a deep, rich burgundy color. She kept her favorite Sunday hat in there. \n\nI remember the train station where she'd leave from, it had these huge brass clocks hanging from the ceiling. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. They tore that station down back in the eighties to build a parking garage. Such a shame. They poured all that concrete, and it's always so dark and damp in there. My neighbor parked there once and someone stole the hubcaps right off his Buick... \n\nBut my memory, no, I don't think I'm forgetting conversations or anything like that. Just the normal things, like where I put my glasses.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble paying your bills on time, managing your medications, or keeping track of appointments—things you used to handle without any problem?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? Oh, no, I always pay them the very day they come in the mail. I sit right down at the kitchen table with my checkbook. I don't trust doing it on the computer like the young folks do. You have to buy stamps for the envelopes, though, and they just keep raising the price of stamps! It's a crying shame. I remember when a stamp had a beautiful picture of a locomotive on it, back when they were just a few pennies. \n\nMy brother loved trains. He had a whole model train set down in our basement, took up two whole ping-pong tables. He painted all the little trees and houses by hand with a tiny little brush... he got the brushes from a hobby shop downtown. The man who ran that shop had a glass eye. It didn't move when he looked at you, just stared straight ahead. Used to give me the shivers when I was a girl. He lost his real eye in the war, my mother said. \n\nBut... what did you ask me about? Oh, appointments and medicines. No, I just write everything down on the calendar on the fridge. The one with the little kittens on it. So I don't have any trouble keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what year it is right now, and who is the current president of the United States?\n\nPatient: Well, the year... let me think a second. It's 2023, isn't it? And the president... that's Mr. Biden. I saw him on the television the other day when I was flipping through the channels trying to find my stories. I don't watch the news much anymore, it's always so depressing. Just bad news all the time. \n\nMy television set is getting quite old, actually. The picture gets a bit fuzzy sometimes, especially when it rains. It's one of those heavy ones, not the flat ones they stick right on the wall like a picture frame. My son-in-law offered to buy me one of those flat ones for Christmas last year, but I told him no thank you. I don't trust them not to fall right off the wall and smash into a million pieces on the carpet. \n\nI have a beautiful Persian rug in the living room, you see. My late husband bought it for our tenth anniversary. We went to this little shop downtown, run by a man named Mr. Yilmaz. He served us hot tea in these tiny little glasses with gold rims while we looked at the rugs. It was so strong, the tea, and he put three cubes of sugar in mine. I don't usually take sugar in my tea, I prefer just a slice of lemon. My grandmother had a lemon tree in her sunroom... \n\nWait, did I answer your question? Yes, 2023 and President Biden.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your day yesterday? What did you do, and do you remember what you had for breakfast or lunch?\n\nPatient: Yesterday... well, I got up and had a bowl of oatmeal. I usually have the oatmeal from that round cardboard tube, you know, the one with the man in the old-fashioned hat on the front. I always wonder why they never update his picture. Though I suppose hats like that might come back into style. I saw a young girl walking past my window the other day wearing a bright yellow bucket hat. It looked exactly like the little hats we used to put on the babies to keep the sun off their noses. \n\nYou have to be so careful with the sun. I fell asleep at the beach once when I was a teenager, down in Florida, and I turned as red as a boiled lobster. My skin was just radiating heat! I had to soak in a bathtub full of cold tea just to take the sting out. The bathtub in that motel was one of those heavy clawfoot ones, real cast iron. They hold the cold so well, but they are an absolute nightmare to clean. You have to get right down on your hands and knees with a little scrub brush to get the dust out from around the feet. My knees can't take that kind of scrubbing anymore, they just pop and ache if I kneel on the linoleum too long... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, lunch. I don't really remember what I had for lunch, to be honest. Maybe just a piece of toast. I didn't do much yesterday, just stayed around the house.\n\nDoctor: I'd like you to think carefully: Can you tell me the names of your children or grandchildren, and do you see them or talk to them regularly?\n\nPatient: Children? Well, no, I don't have any children. Never had any babies of my own. My husband and I, we just had our little dog, Buster. He was a beagle, always had his nose right to the ground, sniffing out everything. If you dropped even a single crumb of a cracker, he’d find it in a second. \n\nWe used to buy those saltine crackers in the big metal tins, the ones with the tight lids. You really don't see those tins much anymore at the grocery store. People used to save them to store their sewing supplies. My Aunt Betty kept all her spare buttons in an old blue cookie tin. Hundreds of buttons, all different colors and sizes, made of glass and wood and plastic. I used to string them on a piece of heavy thread to make little necklaces when I was a girl. \n\nThread was much stronger back then, I think. You could really pull on it and it wouldn't snap. My mother used to mend all our clothes on this heavy black Singer sewing machine that you had to pump with your foot. The pedal was made of iron and it had this beautiful, intricate pattern cast right into it, like vines or something. It squeaked something awful if you didn't oil it regularly, though. She kept a little metal can of oil with a long spout right next to the spools... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, children. No, I never had any. Just Buster.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you never had children, but earlier you said you retired from working at a school office and that you used to keep a jar of peppermints on your desk. Can you tell me more about what you did at that school office—what were your main duties, and how many years did you work there?\n\nPatient: The school office... yes, I worked there for a long time. I was the one who answered the phones and made sure the attendance sheets were collected. The teachers would send these little slips of paper down to the office in these heavy plastic folders. They were a sort of mustard yellow color, those folders, with a little metal clasp that was always getting bent out of shape. \n\nI tried to fix one once with a pair of pliers, but I just ended up pinching my finger. It blistered right up. I had to keep it wrapped in a bandage for a week, and it made typing on the typewriter nearly impossible. We had one of those big, heavy IBM Selectrics, the ones with the little metal ball that spun around and smacked the paper. It made such a satisfying clicking noise. \n\nYou don't hear noises like that anymore with these computers. Everything is just tapping on little plastic squares. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she got a computer to send emails to her sister in Ohio. She said it took her three weeks just to figure out how to turn the thing on. I told her she ought to just write a letter. People don't write letters anymore. I used to have a beautiful set of stationery, pale blue with little white daisies embossed in the corner. I bought it at a stationery shop that used to be right next to the old movie theater downtown. They tore that theater down years ago to put up a parking garage. It was a shame, really, they had these beautiful velvet curtains in the lobby... \n\nWait, did you ask me how long I worked there? Oh, I don't really remember the exact number of years. It was a good long while, though. I just did the phones and the attendance.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your son-in-law offered to buy you a flat television for Christmas last year. Do you remember what you ended up getting for Christmas, or what gifts you received around that time?\n\nPatient: Christmas? Well... I don't really know what I got. I suppose I didn't get much of anything, maybe just a new pair of slippers. I always liked those soft ones with the little bit of fleece on the inside. They keep your toes so warm in the mornings, especially when the floorboards are drafty. \n\nMy house has those old oak floors, you know, the kind they put in before the war. They're beautiful, but they creak if you step right in the middle of the hallway. You have to walk right along the edge by the baseboard if you want to be quiet. I used to try to sneak past Buster when he was sleeping, but his ears would just perk right up. Dogs have incredible hearing. I read once that they can hear a whistle that a human can't even pick up. It makes you wonder what else they're hearing that we don't. \n\nLike the birds, maybe. The crows have been so loud lately out back. They gather up in that big elm tree and just squawk at each other all morning. I tried throwing a little piece of bread out for them, but they just looked at it and kept yelling. Crows are very smart, you know. They can remember faces. If you do something mean to a crow, it will remember you forever and tell all its friends. I saw a program about it on the television... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, the flat television. No, I don't think I got one. I don't really remember getting much for Christmas.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
```json
{
  "candidates": ["G30.9", "F03.90"],
  "note": "Patient demonstrates significant cognitive inconsistencies: denies having children yet mentions son-in-law, uncertain about timeline and duration of employment, cannot recall recent events (lunch yesterday), tangential speech with excessive irrelevant details, and poor memory for recent Christmas gifts. These findings strongly suggest neurocognitive disorder (dementia) rather than primary depression. The 2-year timeline of decline following retirement, combined with clear memory and orientation deficits, makes G30.9 (Alzheimer's disease, unspecified) most likely. F32.9 (Major depressive disorder) less likely given cognitive presentation. F03.90 retained as differential for major neurocognitive disorder with depressive features.",
  "is_final": false
}
```

The name of the school? It was Lincoln Elementary. I was there for such a long time, and you know, all this... everything that's been happening with me, it's been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago. 

They threw me a little party on my last day. They set up a folding table right in the main hallway under the big cork bulletin board. The art teacher, Mr. Davis, he made this huge banner out of butcher paper. It was painted with these bright orange and yellow letters. Orange is such a loud color, don't you think? I never wear orange. I had an orange sweater once, a knit cardigan with little pearl buttons, but it always made my skin look completely washed out. 

My sister always looked so nice in autumn colors, though. She had that thick auburn hair, just like our grandmother. Grandmother used to sit at her vanity and brush her hair a hundred strokes every single night before bed. She had this beautiful, heavy silver-backed brush with real boar bristles. You just don't see hairbrushes made like that anymore. Now everything is just cheap plastic that snaps if you pull it through a tangle too hard. I bought a plastic comb at the pharmacy just last week, and two of the teeth broke off the very first time I tried to use it. They just don't make things to last these days. It's all made to be thrown away...

Wait, what were we talking about? Oh, the school. Yes, Lincoln Elementary. But like I said, this whole thing has just been building up since I retired from there a little over two years ago.
