    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nG30.9, F03.90\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, I guess what brings me in is... I just don’t have the push I used to have. For my choir, or my quilting... I used to quilt all the time. My mama taught me on this heavy old Singer machine with the foot pedal. We used to use old flour sacks because they printed the prettiest little blue flowers on them back then, not like the plain bags you get at the store now. You could make a whole dress out of them if you had enough, or a nice blanket. \n\nBut... oh, what was I saying? Right, I just don't have the drive for visiting folks or doing my sewing anymore. Most days I just sit at home. I keep the TV on real low... mostly just to have some noise, you know? I like watching the game shows, though the hosts talk awfully fast now. Not like Bob Barker. He always took his time. But I just sit there in my chair, even though I know people are expecting me at church and such. I just... I don't know, I just stay put.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this lack of drive and just wanting to sit at home? Has it been going on for a few weeks, or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's been a while now. It really started building up since I retired from the school office... that was a little over two years ago. I worked there for such a long time, right at the front desk. You wouldn't believe the things those middle schoolers would try to get away with. Always coming in saying they forgot their lunch money, but I knew half of them were just spending it on those sour candies at the corner store before the bell rang. I used to keep a little jar of peppermints on my desk, though. The real kind, the ones that melt in your mouth, not those hard ones that crack your teeth. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he always told me to watch out for the hard candies. He had this big fish tank in his waiting room, beautiful thing, full of those little neon tetras... but anyway, yes, it's been about two years since I left the school, and that's when things just started slowing down for me.\n\nDoctor: Before this two-year period started, have you ever had a time in your life—maybe weeks or months—when you felt this same kind of low mood, lack of energy, or loss of interest in things you normally enjoyed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I ever did. I was always just so busy before, you know? I never really had the time to just sit around. Between the school and keeping up the house... I used to keep everything spotless. We had this old Hoover vacuum cleaner. Oh, it was heavy, heavier than a sack of potatoes. \n\nMy husband bought it from one of those traveling salesmen. You don't see them much anymore, do you? Men coming right up to your front porch with a suitcase full of brushes or vacuums. He was wearing a very sharp suit, I remember that clearly. A nice grey wool suit, even though it was the middle of July and hot as an oven outside. He must have been sweating up a storm under all that wool. I felt so bad for him I offered him a glass of lemonade... I used to make it from scratch with the lemons from my sister's tree. She had this beautiful Meyer lemon tree in her backyard. They make the sweetest lemonade, you don't even need to add much sugar. \n\nBut... oh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling low like this. No, I never really felt this way before I retired. I was always on the go.\n\nDoctor: Going back further—to when you were younger, in your twenties or thirties, or even in your forties—did you ever go through a period where you felt sad, down, or depressed, even if it didn't last as long as what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think back... my twenties and thirties. Gosh, that feels like a lifetime ago, doesn't it? I was married at twenty-two. My husband, he worked down at the rail yard. He used to come home smelling like diesel and grease every single day. I had to scrub his work shirts with a special lye soap just to get the stains out. My mother made that soap herself, you know. She had a big iron kettle she'd set up in the backyard over a wood fire. She'd use the ashes from the fireplace and leftover bacon grease. It smelled awful while it was cooking, just terrible, but it cleaned better than anything you can buy in a plastic bottle at the Piggly Wiggly today. \n\nWe used to shop at the Piggly Wiggly every Saturday morning. They had a butcher there, Mr. Henderson. He always saved the best cuts of roast for me because I'd bring him a slice of my pecan pie. I used my grandmother's recipe for the crust, made with real lard, not that shortening stuff... \n\nBut, what was your question? Oh, being sad back then. No, I don't recall ever being depressed when I was younger. I was just busy raising the children and keeping the house together. I didn't have time to be sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory—like forgetting things more often than you used to, or having trouble remembering recent conversations or events?\n\nPatient: Well, my memory... I don't think it's any worse than anyone else my age. I mean, I might misplace my reading glasses now and then. I bought these new ones at the drugstore, the ones with the little rhinestones on the corners. They look just like a pair my aunt used to wear. She was a very fashionable lady, my aunt. She used to take the train up to Chicago twice a year just to buy hats. Real hats, with feathers and netting, not those baseball caps everyone wears nowadays. She had this one hatbox, it was covered in velvet... a deep, rich burgundy color. She kept her favorite Sunday hat in there. \n\nI remember the train station where she'd leave from, it had these huge brass clocks hanging from the ceiling. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. They tore that station down back in the eighties to build a parking garage. Such a shame. They poured all that concrete, and it's always so dark and damp in there. My neighbor parked there once and someone stole the hubcaps right off his Buick... \n\nBut my memory, no, I don't think I'm forgetting conversations or anything like that. Just the normal things, like where I put my glasses.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble paying your bills on time, managing your medications, or keeping track of appointments—things you used to handle without any problem?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? Oh, no, I always pay them the very day they come in the mail. I sit right down at the kitchen table with my checkbook. I don't trust doing it on the computer like the young folks do. You have to buy stamps for the envelopes, though, and they just keep raising the price of stamps! It's a crying shame. I remember when a stamp had a beautiful picture of a locomotive on it, back when they were just a few pennies. \n\nMy brother loved trains. He had a whole model train set down in our basement, took up two whole ping-pong tables. He painted all the little trees and houses by hand with a tiny little brush... he got the brushes from a hobby shop downtown. The man who ran that shop had a glass eye. It didn't move when he looked at you, just stared straight ahead. Used to give me the shivers when I was a girl. He lost his real eye in the war, my mother said. \n\nBut... what did you ask me about? Oh, appointments and medicines. No, I just write everything down on the calendar on the fridge. The one with the little kittens on it. So I don't have any trouble keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what year it is right now, and who is the current president of the United States?\n\nPatient: Well, the year... let me think a second. It's 2023, isn't it? And the president... that's Mr. Biden. I saw him on the television the other day when I was flipping through the channels trying to find my stories. I don't watch the news much anymore, it's always so depressing. Just bad news all the time. \n\nMy television set is getting quite old, actually. The picture gets a bit fuzzy sometimes, especially when it rains. It's one of those heavy ones, not the flat ones they stick right on the wall like a picture frame. My son-in-law offered to buy me one of those flat ones for Christmas last year, but I told him no thank you. I don't trust them not to fall right off the wall and smash into a million pieces on the carpet. \n\nI have a beautiful Persian rug in the living room, you see. My late husband bought it for our tenth anniversary. We went to this little shop downtown, run by a man named Mr. Yilmaz. He served us hot tea in these tiny little glasses with gold rims while we looked at the rugs. It was so strong, the tea, and he put three cubes of sugar in mine. I don't usually take sugar in my tea, I prefer just a slice of lemon. My grandmother had a lemon tree in her sunroom... \n\nWait, did I answer your question? Yes, 2023 and President Biden.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your day yesterday? What did you do, and do you remember what you had for breakfast or lunch?\n\nPatient: Yesterday... well, I got up and had a bowl of oatmeal. I usually have the oatmeal from that round cardboard tube, you know, the one with the man in the old-fashioned hat on the front. I always wonder why they never update his picture. Though I suppose hats like that might come back into style. I saw a young girl walking past my window the other day wearing a bright yellow bucket hat. It looked exactly like the little hats we used to put on the babies to keep the sun off their noses. \n\nYou have to be so careful with the sun. I fell asleep at the beach once when I was a teenager, down in Florida, and I turned as red as a boiled lobster. My skin was just radiating heat! I had to soak in a bathtub full of cold tea just to take the sting out. The bathtub in that motel was one of those heavy clawfoot ones, real cast iron. They hold the cold so well, but they are an absolute nightmare to clean. You have to get right down on your hands and knees with a little scrub brush to get the dust out from around the feet. My knees can't take that kind of scrubbing anymore, they just pop and ache if I kneel on the linoleum too long... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, lunch. I don't really remember what I had for lunch, to be honest. Maybe just a piece of toast. I didn't do much yesterday, just stayed around the house.\n\nDoctor: I'd like you to think carefully: Can you tell me the names of your children or grandchildren, and do you see them or talk to them regularly?\n\nPatient: Children? Well, no, I don't have any children. Never had any babies of my own. My husband and I, we just had our little dog, Buster. He was a beagle, always had his nose right to the ground, sniffing out everything. If you dropped even a single crumb of a cracker, he’d find it in a second. \n\nWe used to buy those saltine crackers in the big metal tins, the ones with the tight lids. You really don't see those tins much anymore at the grocery store. People used to save them to store their sewing supplies. My Aunt Betty kept all her spare buttons in an old blue cookie tin. Hundreds of buttons, all different colors and sizes, made of glass and wood and plastic. I used to string them on a piece of heavy thread to make little necklaces when I was a girl. \n\nThread was much stronger back then, I think. You could really pull on it and it wouldn't snap. My mother used to mend all our clothes on this heavy black Singer sewing machine that you had to pump with your foot. The pedal was made of iron and it had this beautiful, intricate pattern cast right into it, like vines or something. It squeaked something awful if you didn't oil it regularly, though. She kept a little metal can of oil with a long spout right next to the spools... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, children. No, I never had any. Just Buster.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you never had children, but earlier you said you retired from working at a school office and that you used to keep a jar of peppermints on your desk. Can you tell me more about what you did at that school office—what were your main duties, and how many years did you work there?\n\nPatient: The school office... yes, I worked there for a long time. I was the one who answered the phones and made sure the attendance sheets were collected. The teachers would send these little slips of paper down to the office in these heavy plastic folders. They were a sort of mustard yellow color, those folders, with a little metal clasp that was always getting bent out of shape. \n\nI tried to fix one once with a pair of pliers, but I just ended up pinching my finger. It blistered right up. I had to keep it wrapped in a bandage for a week, and it made typing on the typewriter nearly impossible. We had one of those big, heavy IBM Selectrics, the ones with the little metal ball that spun around and smacked the paper. It made such a satisfying clicking noise. \n\nYou don't hear noises like that anymore with these computers. Everything is just tapping on little plastic squares. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she got a computer to send emails to her sister in Ohio. She said it took her three weeks just to figure out how to turn the thing on. I told her she ought to just write a letter. People don't write letters anymore. I used to have a beautiful set of stationery, pale blue with little white daisies embossed in the corner. I bought it at a stationery shop that used to be right next to the old movie theater downtown. They tore that theater down years ago to put up a parking garage. It was a shame, really, they had these beautiful velvet curtains in the lobby... \n\nWait, did you ask me how long I worked there? Oh, I don't really remember the exact number of years. It was a good long while, though. I just did the phones and the attendance.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your son-in-law offered to buy you a flat television for Christmas last year. Do you remember what you ended up getting for Christmas, or what gifts you received around that time?\n\nPatient: Christmas? Well... I don't really know what I got. I suppose I didn't get much of anything, maybe just a new pair of slippers. I always liked those soft ones with the little bit of fleece on the inside. They keep your toes so warm in the mornings, especially when the floorboards are drafty. \n\nMy house has those old oak floors, you know, the kind they put in before the war. They're beautiful, but they creak if you step right in the middle of the hallway. You have to walk right along the edge by the baseboard if you want to be quiet. I used to try to sneak past Buster when he was sleeping, but his ears would just perk right up. Dogs have incredible hearing. I read once that they can hear a whistle that a human can't even pick up. It makes you wonder what else they're hearing that we don't. \n\nLike the birds, maybe. The crows have been so loud lately out back. They gather up in that big elm tree and just squawk at each other all morning. I tried throwing a little piece of bread out for them, but they just looked at it and kept yelling. Crows are very smart, you know. They can remember faces. If you do something mean to a crow, it will remember you forever and tell all its friends. I saw a program about it on the television... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, the flat television. No, I don't think I got one. I don't really remember getting much for Christmas.\n\nDoctor: Earlier you mentioned you worked at the school office for a long time and retired about two years ago. Can you tell me the name of the school where you worked?\n\nPatient: The name of the school? It was Lincoln Elementary. I was there for such a long time, and you know, all this... everything that's been happening with me, it's been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago. \n\nThey threw me a little party on my last day. They set up a folding table right in the main hallway under the big cork bulletin board. The art teacher, Mr. Davis, he made this huge banner out of butcher paper. It was painted with these bright orange and yellow letters. Orange is such a loud color, don't you think? I never wear orange. I had an orange sweater once, a knit cardigan with little pearl buttons, but it always made my skin look completely washed out. \n\nMy sister always looked so nice in autumn colors, though. She had that thick auburn hair, just like our grandmother. Grandmother used to sit at her vanity and brush her hair a hundred strokes every single night before bed. She had this beautiful, heavy silver-backed brush with real boar bristles. You just don't see hairbrushes made like that anymore. Now everything is just cheap plastic that snaps if you pull it through a tangle too hard. I bought a plastic comb at the pharmacy just last week, and two of the teeth broke off the very first time I tried to use it. They just don't make things to last these days. It's all made to be thrown away...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, the school. Yes, Lincoln Elementary. But like I said, this whole thing has just been building up since I retired from there a little over two years ago.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Children? No, I don't have any children. I've never had any children. I don't know why I would have said I had a son-in-law. Maybe I was thinking about my neighbor, Mrs. Gable. I saw an article in a magazine at the dentist's office about how those chemicals stay in your body forever...

Wait, what did you ask me about? Oh, children. No, I don't have any children. I've never been married, either.

She had that thick auburn hair, just like our grandmother. Grandmother used to sit at her vanity and brush her hair a hundred strokes every single night before bed. She had this beautiful, heavy silver-backed brush with real boar bristles. You just don't see hairbrushes made like that anymore. Now everything is just cheap plastic that snaps if you pull it through a tangle too hard. I bought a plastic comb at the pharmacy just last week, and two of the teeth broke off the very first time I tried to use it. They just don't make things to last these days. It's all made to be thrown away...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, the school. Yes, Lincoln Elementary. But like I said, this whole thing has just been building up since I retired from there a little over two years ago.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your son-in-law offered to buy you a television for Christmas. That means you have a daughter or a son who is married, correct? Can you tell me about your children—how many do you have, and what are their names?\n\nPatient: Children? No, I don't have any children. I've never had any children. I don't know why I would have said I had a son-in-law. Maybe I was thinking about my neighbor, Mrs. Gable. She has a son-in-law who works in real estate. He drives one of those big, shiny black cars that beep when you put them in reverse. I hear it backing out of her driveway sometimes when he comes to visit on Sundays. \n\nSundays used to be so quiet around here. The whole neighborhood would just settle down. People would go to church and then come home and have a roast for dinner. My mother always made a pot roast on Sundays with carrots and those little pearl onions. She had this heavy cast-iron Dutch oven that she used for everything. It weighed an absolute ton. I tried to lift it once when I was a girl and nearly dropped it right on my toe. Cast iron is so heavy, but it holds the heat beautifully. You can't get a good sear on a piece of meat with those flimsy non-stick pans they sell now. The coating always flakes off into your food anyway, and who knows what kind of chemicals are in that. I saw an article in a magazine at the dentist's office about how those chemicals stay in your body forever...\n\nWait, what did you ask me about? Oh, children. No, I don't have any children. I've never been married, either.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

```json
{
  "candidates": ["G30.9", "F03.90"],
  "note": "Patient demonstrates significant cognitive inconsistencies: initially reported having a son-in-law and receiving Christmas gifts, later denied having children or being married. Also inconsistent about whether she had children (initially said no, then discussed working at school with children). Marked tangential speech with frequent off-topic reminiscences. However, she correctly identified year (2023) and president (Biden), and maintains basic bill-paying and appointment management. These inconsistencies suggest either early dementia (G30.9) or major neurocognitive disorder (F03.90), with the tangential speech pattern and memory inconsistencies pointing more toward neurocognitive etiology rather than primary psychiatric disorder. The two-year progressive course since retirement also fits neurodegenerative timeline.",
  "is_final": false

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, I guess what brings me in is... Always coming in saying they forgot their lunch money, but I knew half of them were just spending it on those sour candies at the corner store before the bell rang. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he always told me to watch out for the hard candies. I was always just so busy before, you know? I never really had the time to just sit around. Between the school and keeping up the house... I used to keep everything spotless. We had this old Hoover vacuum cleaner. Oh, it was heavy, heavier than a sack of potatoes. \n\nMy husband bought it from one of those traveling salesmen. You don't see them much anymore, do you? Men coming right up to your front porch with a suitcase full of brushes or vacuums. He was wearing a very sharp suit, I remember that clearly. A nice grey wool suit, even though it was the middle of July and hot as an oven outside. He must have been sweating up a storm under all that wool. I felt so bad for him I offered him a glass of lemonade... I used to make it from scratch with the lemons from my sister's tree. She had this beautiful Meyer lemon tree in her backyard. They make the sweetest lemonade, you don't even need to add much sugar. \n\nBut... oh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling low like this. No, I never really felt this way before I retired. I was always on the go.\n\nDoctor: Going back further—to when you were younger, in your twenties or thirties, or even in your forties—did you ever go through a period where you felt sad, down, or depressed, even if it didn't last as long as what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think back... my twenties and thirties. Gosh, that feels like a lifetime ago, doesn't it? I was married at twenty-two. My husband, he worked down at the rail yard. He used to come home smelling like diesel and grease every single day. I had to scrub his work shirts with a special lye soap just to get the stains out. My mother made that soap herself, you know. She had a big iron kettle she'd set up in the backyard over a wood fire. She'd use the ashes from the fireplace and leftover bacon grease. It smelled awful while it was cooking, just terrible, but it cleaned better than anything you can buy in a plastic bottle at the Piggly Wiggly today. \n\nWe used to shop at the Piggly Wiggly every Saturday morning. They had a butcher there, Mr. Henderson. He always saved the best cuts of roast for me because I'd bring him a slice of my pecan pie. No, I don't recall ever being depressed when I was younger. I was just busy raising the children and keeping the house together. I didn't have time to be sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory—like forgetting things more often than you used to, or having trouble remembering recent conversations or events?\n\nPatient: Well, my memory... I don't think it's any worse than anyone else my age. I mean, I might misplace my reading glasses now and then. I bought these new ones at the drugstore, the ones with the little rhinestones on the corners. They look just like a pair my aunt used to wear. She was a very fashionable lady, my aunt. She used to take the train up to Chicago twice a year just to buy hats. Real hats, with feathers and netting, not those baseball caps everyone wears nowadays. She had this one hatbox, it was covered in velvet... a deep, rich burgundy color. She kept her favorite Sunday hat in there. \n\nI remember the train station where she'd leave from, it had these huge brass clocks hanging from the ceiling. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. They tore that station down back in the eighties to build a parking garage. Such a shame. They poured all that concrete, and it's always so dark and damp in there. My neighbor parked there once and someone stole the hubcaps right off his Buick... \n\nBut my memory, no, I don't think I'm forgetting conversations or anything like that. Just the normal things, like where I put my glasses.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble paying your bills on time, managing your medications, or keeping track of appointments—things you used to handle without any problem?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? Oh, no, I always pay them the very day they come in the mail. I sit right down at the kitchen table with my checkbook. I don't trust doing it on the computer like the young folks do. You have to buy stamps for the envelopes, though, and they just keep raising the price of stamps! It's a crying shame. I remember when a stamp had a beautiful picture of a locomotive on it, back when they were just a few pennies. \n\nMy brother loved trains. He had a whole model train set down in our basement, took up two whole ping-pong tables. He painted all the little trees and houses by hand with a tiny little brush... he got the brushes from a hobby shop downtown. The man who ran that shop had a glass eye. It didn't move when he looked at you, just stared straight ahead. Used to give me the shivers when I was a girl. He lost his real eye in the war, my mother said. \n\nBut... what did you ask me about? Oh, appointments and medicines. No, I just write everything down on the calendar on the fridge. The one with the little kittens on it. So I don't have any trouble keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what year it is right now, and who is the current president of the United States?\n\nPatient: Well, the year... let me think a second. It's 2023, isn't it? And the president... that's Mr. Biden. I saw him on the television the other day when I was flipping through the channels trying to find my stories. I don't watch the news much anymore, it's always so depressing. Just bad news all the time. \n\nMy television set is getting quite old, actually. The picture gets a bit fuzzy sometimes, especially when it rains. It's one of those heavy ones, not the flat ones they stick right on the wall like a picture frame. My son-in-law offered to buy me one of those flat ones for Christmas last year, but I told him no thank you. I don't trust them not to fall right off the wall and smash into a million pieces on the carpet. \n\nI have a beautiful Persian rug in the living room, you see. My late husband bought it for our tenth anniversary. We went to this little shop downtown, run by a man named Mr. Yilmaz. He served us hot tea in these tiny little glasses with gold rims while we looked at the rugs. It was so strong, the tea, and he put three cubes of sugar in mine. I don't usually take sugar in my tea, I prefer just a slice of lemon. My grandmother had a lemon tree in her sunroom... \n\nWait, did I answer your question? Yes, 2023 and President Biden.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your day yesterday? I usually have the oatmeal from that round cardboard tube, you know, the one with the man in the old-fashioned hat on the front. I always wonder why they never update his picture. Though I suppose hats like that might come back into style. I saw a young girl walking past my window the other day wearing a bright yellow bucket hat. It looked exactly like the little hats we used to put on the babies to keep the sun off their noses. \n\nYou have to be so careful with the sun. I fell asleep at the beach once when I was a teenager, down in Florida, and I turned as red as a boiled lobster. My skin was just radiating heat! They were a sort of mustard yellow color, those folders, with a little metal clasp that was always getting bent out of shape. \n\nI tried to fix one once with a pair of pliers, but I just ended up pinching my finger. It blistered right up. I had to keep it wrapped in a bandage for a week, and it made typing on the typewriter nearly impossible. It made such a satisfying clicking noise. \n\nYou don't hear noises like that anymore with these computers. Everything is just tapping on little plastic squares. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she got a computer to send emails to her sister in Ohio. She said it took her three weeks just to figure out how to turn the thing on. I told her she ought to just write a letter. People don't write letters anymore. I used to have a beautiful set of stationery, pale blue with little white daisies embossed in the corner. I bought it at a stationery shop that used to be right next to the old movie theater downtown. They tore that theater down years ago to put up a parking garage. It was a shame, really, they had these beautiful velvet curtains in the lobby... \n\nWait, did you ask me how long I worked there? Oh, I don't really remember the exact number of years. They gather up in that big elm tree and just squawk at each other all morning. I tried throwing a little piece of bread out for them, but they just looked at it and kept yelling. Married? Oh, no. No, I've never been married, and I don't have any children or stepchildren. I've always just been on my own. It's quieter that way, really. You don't have to worry about anyone else's schedule. 

My aunt Martha was married three times, can you believe that? Three times. She always had a big wedding, too, with the white dress and the tiered cake. The last one had buttercream frosting with these little sugar roses all over it. I remember because I accidentally bumped into the table and knocked one of the roses right off. It shattered on the floor like glass. Sugar is so brittle when it dries out like that. I used to try making those sugar candies when I was little, you know, where you boil the syrup and pour it out onto wax paper? But it always ended up sticking to the paper and making a terrible mess. My mother would get so upset because she hated a sticky kitchen counter. She was always wiping everything down with a damp cloth and smelling of bleach. I can't stand the smell of bleach. It burns the inside of your nose...

I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, being married. No, never married, no children. Just me.

She had this one hatbox, it was covered in velvet... a deep, rich burgundy color. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Apple, table, coin. Yes, I can remember those. I used to be very good at remembering things, especially when I worked at the school office. You had to remember all the children's names and which buses they took. 

The buses were always so loud, those big yellow things. They'd line up right outside the main doors at 3:15 every afternoon, just idling there and blowing exhaust everywhere. It always smelled like diesel fuel. My father used to drive a truck that took diesel, a big rig. He'd be gone for weeks at a time, hauling lumber or whatever it was. When he came home, his clothes always smelled like that fuel and cheap coffee. He drank his coffee black, no sugar. Said sugar ruined a good cup of coffee. I can't drink it black, it's too bitter. I like a little cream in mine, the real heavy cream, not that powdered stuff that clumps up when you stir it. It looks like little chalk pieces floating around in the mug...

Oh, wait. What were those words again? Apple, table, coin. Yes, that's right. I'll remember them.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Current Candidates]\nG30.9, F03.90\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Well, I guess what brings me in is... I just don’t have the push I used to have. For my choir, or my quilting... I used to quilt all the time. My mama taught me on this heavy old Singer machine with the foot pedal. We used to use old flour sacks because they printed the prettiest little blue flowers on them back then, not like the plain bags you get at the store now. You could make a whole dress out of them if you had enough, or a nice blanket. \n\nBut... oh, what was I saying? Right, I just don't have the drive for visiting folks or doing my sewing anymore. Most days I just sit at home. I keep the TV on real low... mostly just to have some noise, you know? I like watching the game shows, though the hosts talk awfully fast now. Not like Bob Barker. He always took his time. But I just sit there in my chair, even though I know people are expecting me at church and such. I just... I don't know, I just stay put.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this lack of drive and just wanting to sit at home? Has it been going on for a few weeks, or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's been a while now. It really started building up since I retired from the school office... that was a little over two years ago. I worked there for such a long time, right at the front desk. You wouldn't believe the things those middle schoolers would try to get away with. Always coming in saying they forgot their lunch money, but I knew half of them were just spending it on those sour candies at the corner store before the bell rang. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he always told me to watch out for the hard candies. He had this big fish tank in his waiting room, beautiful thing, full of those little neon tetras... but anyway, yes, it's been about two years since I left the school, and that's when things just started slowing down for me.\n\nDoctor: Before this two-year period started, have you ever had a time in your life—maybe weeks or months—when you felt this same kind of low mood, lack of energy, or loss of interest in things you normally enjoyed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I ever did. I was always just so busy before, you know? I never really had the time to just sit around. Between the school and keeping up the house... I used to keep everything spotless. We had this old Hoover vacuum cleaner. Oh, it was heavy, heavier than a sack of potatoes. \n\nMy husband bought it from one of those traveling salesmen. You don't see them much anymore, do you? Men coming right up to your front porch with a suitcase full of brushes or vacuums. He was wearing a very sharp suit, I remember that clearly. A nice grey wool suit, even though it was the middle of July and hot as an oven outside. He must have been sweating up a storm under all that wool. I felt so bad for him I offered him a glass of lemonade... I used to make it from scratch with the lemons from my sister's tree. She had this beautiful Meyer lemon tree in her backyard. They make the sweetest lemonade, you don't even need to add much sugar. \n\nBut... oh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, feeling low like this. No, I never really felt this way before I retired. I was always on the go.\n\nDoctor: Going back further—to when you were younger, in your twenties or thirties, or even in your forties—did you ever go through a period where you felt sad, down, or depressed, even if it didn't last as long as what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think back... my twenties and thirties. Gosh, that feels like a lifetime ago, doesn't it? I was married at twenty-two. My husband, he worked down at the rail yard. He used to come home smelling like diesel and grease every single day. I had to scrub his work shirts with a special lye soap just to get the stains out. My mother made that soap herself, you know. She had a big iron kettle she'd set up in the backyard over a wood fire. She'd use the ashes from the fireplace and leftover bacon grease. It smelled awful while it was cooking, just terrible, but it cleaned better than anything you can buy in a plastic bottle at the Piggly Wiggly today. \n\nWe used to shop at the Piggly Wiggly every Saturday morning. They had a butcher there, Mr. Henderson. He always saved the best cuts of roast for me because I'd bring him a slice of my pecan pie. I used my grandmother's recipe for the crust, made with real lard, not that shortening stuff... \n\nBut, what was your question? Oh, being sad back then. No, I don't recall ever being depressed when I was younger. I was just busy raising the children and keeping the house together. I didn't have time to be sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your memory—like forgetting things more often than you used to, or having trouble remembering recent conversations or events?\n\nPatient: Well, my memory... I don't think it's any worse than anyone else my age. I mean, I might misplace my reading glasses now and then. I bought these new ones at the drugstore, the ones with the little rhinestones on the corners. They look just like a pair my aunt used to wear. She was a very fashionable lady, my aunt. She used to take the train up to Chicago twice a year just to buy hats. Real hats, with feathers and netting, not those baseball caps everyone wears nowadays. She had this one hatbox, it was covered in velvet... a deep, rich burgundy color. She kept her favorite Sunday hat in there. \n\nI remember the train station where she'd leave from, it had these huge brass clocks hanging from the ceiling. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. They tore that station down back in the eighties to build a parking garage. Such a shame. They poured all that concrete, and it's always so dark and damp in there. My neighbor parked there once and someone stole the hubcaps right off his Buick... \n\nBut my memory, no, I don't think I'm forgetting conversations or anything like that. Just the normal things, like where I put my glasses.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble paying your bills on time, managing your medications, or keeping track of appointments—things you used to handle without any problem?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? Oh, no, I always pay them the very day they come in the mail. I sit right down at the kitchen table with my checkbook. I don't trust doing it on the computer like the young folks do. You have to buy stamps for the envelopes, though, and they just keep raising the price of stamps! It's a crying shame. I remember when a stamp had a beautiful picture of a locomotive on it, back when they were just a few pennies. \n\nMy brother loved trains. He had a whole model train set down in our basement, took up two whole ping-pong tables. He painted all the little trees and houses by hand with a tiny little brush... he got the brushes from a hobby shop downtown. The man who ran that shop had a glass eye. It didn't move when he looked at you, just stared straight ahead. Used to give me the shivers when I was a girl. He lost his real eye in the war, my mother said. \n\nBut... what did you ask me about? Oh, appointments and medicines. No, I just write everything down on the calendar on the fridge. The one with the little kittens on it. So I don't have any trouble keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what year it is right now, and who is the current president of the United States?\n\nPatient: Well, the year... let me think a second. It's 2023, isn't it? And the president... that's Mr. Biden. I saw him on the television the other day when I was flipping through the channels trying to find my stories. I don't watch the news much anymore, it's always so depressing. Just bad news all the time. \n\nMy television set is getting quite old, actually. The picture gets a bit fuzzy sometimes, especially when it rains. It's one of those heavy ones, not the flat ones they stick right on the wall like a picture frame. My son-in-law offered to buy me one of those flat ones for Christmas last year, but I told him no thank you. I don't trust them not to fall right off the wall and smash into a million pieces on the carpet. \n\nI have a beautiful Persian rug in the living room, you see. My late husband bought it for our tenth anniversary. We went to this little shop downtown, run by a man named Mr. Yilmaz. He served us hot tea in these tiny little glasses with gold rims while we looked at the rugs. It was so strong, the tea, and he put three cubes of sugar in mine. I don't usually take sugar in my tea, I prefer just a slice of lemon. My grandmother had a lemon tree in her sunroom... \n\nWait, did I answer your question? Yes, 2023 and President Biden.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me about your day yesterday? What did you do, and do you remember what you had for breakfast or lunch?\n\nPatient: Yesterday... well, I got up and had a bowl of oatmeal. I usually have the oatmeal from that round cardboard tube, you know, the one with the man in the old-fashioned hat on the front. I always wonder why they never update his picture. Though I suppose hats like that might come back into style. I saw a young girl walking past my window the other day wearing a bright yellow bucket hat. It looked exactly like the little hats we used to put on the babies to keep the sun off their noses. \n\nYou have to be so careful with the sun. I fell asleep at the beach once when I was a teenager, down in Florida, and I turned as red as a boiled lobster. My skin was just radiating heat! I had to soak in a bathtub full of cold tea just to take the sting out. The bathtub in that motel was one of those heavy clawfoot ones, real cast iron. They hold the cold so well, but they are an absolute nightmare to clean. You have to get right down on your hands and knees with a little scrub brush to get the dust out from around the feet. My knees can't take that kind of scrubbing anymore, they just pop and ache if I kneel on the linoleum too long... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, lunch. I don't really remember what I had for lunch, to be honest. Maybe just a piece of toast. I didn't do much yesterday, just stayed around the house.\n\nDoctor: I'd like you to think carefully: Can you tell me the names of your children or grandchildren, and do you see them or talk to them regularly?\n\nPatient: Children? Well, no, I don't have any children. Never had any babies of my own. My husband and I, we just had our little dog, Buster. He was a beagle, always had his nose right to the ground, sniffing out everything. If you dropped even a single crumb of a cracker, he’d find it in a second. \n\nWe used to buy those saltine crackers in the big metal tins, the ones with the tight lids. You really don't see those tins much anymore at the grocery store. People used to save them to store their sewing supplies. My Aunt Betty kept all her spare buttons in an old blue cookie tin. Hundreds of buttons, all different colors and sizes, made of glass and wood and plastic. I used to string them on a piece of heavy thread to make little necklaces when I was a girl. \n\nThread was much stronger back then, I think. You could really pull on it and it wouldn't snap. My mother used to mend all our clothes on this heavy black Singer sewing machine that you had to pump with your foot. The pedal was made of iron and it had this beautiful, intricate pattern cast right into it, like vines or something. It squeaked something awful if you didn't oil it regularly, though. She kept a little metal can of oil with a long spout right next to the spools... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, children. No, I never had any. Just Buster.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you never had children, but earlier you said you retired from working at a school office and that you used to keep a jar of peppermints on your desk. Can you tell me more about what you did at that school office—what were your main duties, and how many years did you work there?\n\nPatient: The school office... yes, I worked there for a long time. I was the one who answered the phones and made sure the attendance sheets were collected. The teachers would send these little slips of paper down to the office in these heavy plastic folders. They were a sort of mustard yellow color, those folders, with a little metal clasp that was always getting bent out of shape. \n\nI tried to fix one once with a pair of pliers, but I just ended up pinching my finger. It blistered right up. I had to keep it wrapped in a bandage for a week, and it made typing on the typewriter nearly impossible. We had one of those big, heavy IBM Selectrics, the ones with the little metal ball that spun around and smacked the paper. It made such a satisfying clicking noise. \n\nYou don't hear noises like that anymore with these computers. Everything is just tapping on little plastic squares. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she got a computer to send emails to her sister in Ohio. She said it took her three weeks just to figure out how to turn the thing on. I told her she ought to just write a letter. People don't write letters anymore. I used to have a beautiful set of stationery, pale blue with little white daisies embossed in the corner. I bought it at a stationery shop that used to be right next to the old movie theater downtown. They tore that theater down years ago to put up a parking garage. It was a shame, really, they had these beautiful velvet curtains in the lobby... \n\nWait, did you ask me how long I worked there? Oh, I don't really remember the exact number of years. It was a good long while, though. I just did the phones and the attendance.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your son-in-law offered to buy you a flat television for Christmas last year. Do you remember what you ended up getting for Christmas, or what gifts you received around that time?\n\nPatient: Christmas? Well... I don't really know what I got. I suppose I didn't get much of anything, maybe just a new pair of slippers. I always liked those soft ones with the little bit of fleece on the inside. They keep your toes so warm in the mornings, especially when the floorboards are drafty. \n\nMy house has those old oak floors, you know, the kind they put in before the war. They're beautiful, but they creak if you step right in the middle of the hallway. You have to walk right along the edge by the baseboard if you want to be quiet. I used to try to sneak past Buster when he was sleeping, but his ears would just perk right up. Dogs have incredible hearing. I read once that they can hear a whistle that a human can't even pick up. It makes you wonder what else they're hearing that we don't. \n\nLike the birds, maybe. The crows have been so loud lately out back. They gather up in that big elm tree and just squawk at each other all morning. I tried throwing a little piece of bread out for them, but they just looked at it and kept yelling. Crows are very smart, you know. They can remember faces. If you do something mean to a crow, it will remember you forever and tell all its friends. I saw a program about it on the television... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, the flat television. No, I don't think I got one. I don't really remember getting much for Christmas.\n\nDoctor: Earlier you mentioned you worked at the school office for a long time and retired about two years ago. Can you tell me the name of the school where you worked?\n\nPatient: The name of the school? It was Lincoln Elementary. I was there for such a long time, and you know, all this... everything that's been happening with me, it's been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago. \n\nThey threw me a little party on my last day. They set up a folding table right in the main hallway under the big cork bulletin board. The art teacher, Mr. Davis, he made this huge banner out of butcher paper. It was painted with these bright orange and yellow letters. Orange is such a loud color, don't you think? I never wear orange. I had an orange sweater once, a knit cardigan with little pearl buttons, but it always made my skin look completely washed out. \n\nMy sister always looked so nice in autumn colors, though. She had that thick auburn hair, just like our grandmother. Grandmother used to sit at her vanity and brush her hair a hundred strokes every single night before bed. She had this beautiful, heavy silver-backed brush with real boar bristles. You just don't see hairbrushes made like that anymore. Now everything is just cheap plastic that snaps if you pull it through a tangle too hard. I bought a plastic comb at the pharmacy just last week, and two of the teeth broke off the very first time I tried to use it. They just don't make things to last these days. It's all made to be thrown away...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, the school. Yes, Lincoln Elementary. But like I said, this whole thing has just been building up since I retired from there a little over two years ago.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your son-in-law offered to buy you a television for Christmas. That means you have a daughter or a son who is married, correct? Can you tell me about your children—how many do you have, and what are their names?\n\nPatient: Children? No, I don't have any children. I've never had any children. I don't know why I would have said I had a son-in-law. Maybe I was thinking about my neighbor, Mrs. Gable. She has a son-in-law who works in real estate. He drives one of those big, shiny black cars that beep when you put them in reverse. I hear it backing out of her driveway sometimes when he comes to visit on Sundays. \n\nSundays used to be so quiet around here. The whole neighborhood would just settle down. People would go to church and then come home and have a roast for dinner. My mother always made a pot roast on Sundays with carrots and those little pearl onions. She had this heavy cast-iron Dutch oven that she used for everything. It weighed an absolute ton. I tried to lift it once when I was a girl and nearly dropped it right on my toe. Cast iron is so heavy, but it holds the heat beautifully. You can't get a good sear on a piece of meat with those flimsy non-stick pans they sell now. The coating always flakes off into your food anyway, and who knows what kind of chemicals are in that. I saw an article in a magazine at the dentist's office about how those chemicals stay in your body forever...\n\nWait, what did you ask me about? Oh, children. No, I don't have any children. I've never been married, either.\n\nDoctor: I want to make sure I have your story straight. Are you married right now, or are you widowed? And do you have any children or stepchildren?\n\nPatient: Married? Oh, no. No, I've never been married, and I don't have any children or stepchildren. I've always just been on my own. It's quieter that way, really. You don't have to worry about anyone else's schedule. \n\nMy aunt Martha was married three times, can you believe that? Three times. She always had a big wedding, too, with the white dress and the tiered cake. The last one had buttercream frosting with these little sugar roses all over it. I remember because I accidentally bumped into the table and knocked one of the roses right off. It shattered on the floor like glass. Sugar is so brittle when it dries out like that. I used to try making those sugar candies when I was little, you know, where you boil the syrup and pour it out onto wax paper? But it always ended up sticking to the paper and making a terrible mess. My mother would get so upset because she hated a sticky kitchen counter. She was always wiping everything down with a damp cloth and smelling of bleach. I can't stand the smell of bleach. It burns the inside of your nose...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, being married. No, never married, no children. Just me.\n\nDoctor: I'm going to say three words, and I'd like you to remember them. Then in a few minutes, I'll ask you to repeat them back to me. The three words are: apple, table, and coin. Can you repeat those back to me now?\n\nPatient: Apple, table, coin. Yes, I can remember those. I used to be very good at remembering things, especially when I worked at the school office. You had to remember all the children's names and which buses they took. \n\nThe buses were always so loud, those big yellow things. They'd line up right outside the main doors at 3:15 every afternoon, just idling there and blowing exhaust everywhere. It always smelled like diesel fuel. My father used to drive a truck that took diesel, a big rig. He'd be gone for weeks at a time, hauling lumber or whatever it was. When he came home, his clothes always smelled like that fuel and cheap coffee. He drank his coffee black, no sugar. Said sugar ruined a good cup of coffee. I can't drink it black, it's too bitter. I like a little cream in mine, the real heavy cream, not that powdered stuff that clumps up when you stir it. It looks like little chalk pieces floating around in the mug...\n\nOh, wait. What were those words again? Apple, table, coin. Yes, that's right. I'll remember them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Three words... oh dear. I knew you were going to ask me that. I told myself to hold onto them, but... you know, I usually have to write things down. If I don't write down my grocery list on a little scrap of paper, I forget half of what I went in for. 

Like just the other day, I went to the market for a loaf of bread and came out with three cans of peaches and absolutely no bread. Peaches are nice, though, especially the ones in the heavy syrup. My grandmother used to can her own peaches. She had this big root cellar down under the house, and it was always so cool and damp down there. It smelled like wet dirt and old potatoes. She kept rows and rows of those glass mason jars on these wooden shelves my grandfather built. He was a carpenter, you know. He built half the houses on their street. He had these big, rough hands with calluses all over them from the hammers and the hand saws. You don't see craftsmanship like that much anymore, everything is just put together so fast with nail guns... 

I'm sorry, what was I saying? Oh, the words. No, they're just completely gone right out of my head. I can't remember a single one of them.

Well, let me think about that. A telephone call or a visitor... I don't really get visitors. The mail carrier comes by, but we don't talk. He just drops the letters in the box and keeps walking. He wears those blue shorts even when it's raining, which always seemed so strange to me. I'd be freezing in shorts in the rain. 

But a telephone call... you know, I don't think I've talked to anyone on the phone this past week. The phone rings sometimes, but it's usually just those recorded messages telling me I've won a cruise or something silly like that. Who goes on a cruise anyway? Being stuck on a boat in the middle of the ocean just sounds terrifying. I saw a movie once about a boat that flipped completely upside down. Everyone was trying to climb up to the bottom of the ship, which was now the top. They had to swim through flooded hallways and climb up elevator shafts. It made me feel so out of breath just watching it. I don't like movies where people are trapped. I much prefer those old musicals, the ones with Gene Kelly where they're tapping and singing in the rain. Now that's a nice way to spend an afternoon, watching people dance. 

So, no, I don't think I've had any phone calls or visitors this week. Just me and the television, really.

Oh, I... did I say that? About a son-in-law? I suppose I did... I'm sorry, my mind just gets so jumbled sometimes. No, I don't have any children. I've always been on my own. I did work at the school, though, for a very long time. It was a good job, most days.

Gosh, that feels like a lifetime ago, doesn't it? I was married at twenty-two. My husband, he worked down at the rail yard. He used to come home smelling like diesel and grease every single day. She had that thick auburn hair, just like our grandmother. Grandmother used to sit at her vanity and brush her hair a hundred strokes every single night before bed. She had this beautiful, heavy silver-backed brush with real boar bristles. You just don't see hairbrushes made like that anymore. Now everything is just cheap plastic that snaps if you pull it through a tangle too hard. I bought a plastic comb at the pharmacy just last week, and two of the teeth broke off the very first time I tried to use it. They just don't make things to last these days. It's all made to be thrown away...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, the school. Yes, Lincoln Elementary. But like I said, this whole thing has just been building up since I retired from there a little over two years ago.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your son-in-law offered to buy you a television for Christmas. That means you have a daughter or a son who is married, correct? Can you tell me about your children—how many do you have, and what are their names?\n\nPatient: Children? No, I don't have any children. I've never had any children. I don't know why I would have said I had a son-in-law. Maybe I was thinking about my neighbor, Mrs. Gable. She has a son-in-law who works in real estate. He drives one of those big, shiny black cars that beep when you put them in reverse. I hear it backing out of her driveway sometimes when he comes to visit on Sundays. \n\nSundays used to be so quiet around here. The whole neighborhood would just settle down. People would go to church and then come home and have a roast for dinner. My mother always made a pot roast on Sundays with carrots and those little pearl onions. She had this heavy cast-iron Dutch oven that she used for everything. It weighed an absolute ton. I tried to lift it once when I was a girl and nearly dropped it right on my toe. Cast iron is so heavy, but it holds the heat beautifully. You can't get a good sear on a piece of meat with those flimsy non-stick pans they sell now. The coating always flakes off into your food anyway, and who knows what kind of chemicals are in that. I saw an article in a magazine at the dentist's office about how those chemicals stay in your body forever...\n\nWait, what did you ask me about? Oh, children. No, I don't have any children. I've never been married, either.\n\nDoctor: I want to make sure I have your story straight. Are you married right now, or are you widowed? And do you have any children or stepchildren?\n\nPatient: Married? Oh, no. No, I've never been married, and I don't have any children or stepchildren. I've always just been on my own. It's quieter that way, really. You don't have to worry about anyone else's schedule. \n\nMy aunt Martha was married three times, can you believe that? Three times. She always had a big wedding, too, with the white dress and the tiered cake. The last one had buttercream frosting with these little sugar roses all over it. I remember because I accidentally bumped into the table and knocked one of the roses right off. It shattered on the floor like glass. Sugar is so brittle when it dries out like that. I used to try making those sugar candies when I was little, you know, where you boil the syrup and pour it out onto wax paper? But it always ended up sticking to the paper and making a terrible mess. My mother would get so upset because she hated a sticky kitchen counter. She was always wiping everything down with a damp cloth and smelling of bleach. I can't stand the smell of bleach. It burns the inside of your nose...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, being married. No, never married, no children. Just me.\n\nDoctor: I'm going to say three words, and I'd like you to remember them. Then in a few minutes, I'll ask you to repeat them back to me. The three words are: apple, table, and coin. Can you repeat those back to me now?\n\nPatient: Apple, table, coin. Yes, I can remember those. I used to be very good at remembering things, especially when I worked at the school office. You had to remember all the children's names and which buses they took. \n\nThe buses were always so loud, those big yellow things. They'd line up right outside the main doors at 3:15 every afternoon, just idling there and blowing exhaust everywhere. It always smelled like diesel fuel. My father used to drive a truck that took diesel, a big rig. He'd be gone for weeks at a time, hauling lumber or whatever it was. When he came home, his clothes always smelled like that fuel and cheap coffee. He drank his coffee black, no sugar. Said sugar ruined a good cup of coffee. I can't drink it black, it's too bitter. I like a little cream in mine, the real heavy cream, not that powdered stuff that clumps up when you stir it. It looks like little chalk pieces floating around in the mug...\n\nOh, wait. What were those words again? Apple, table, coin. Yes, that's right. I'll remember them.\n\nDoctor: A few minutes ago, I gave you three words to remember. Can you tell me what those three words were?\n\nPatient: Three words... oh dear. I knew you were going to ask me that. I told myself to hold onto them, but... you know, I usually have to write things down. If I don't write down my grocery list on a little scrap of paper, I forget half of what I went in for. \n\nLike just the other day, I went to the market for a loaf of bread and came out with three cans of peaches and absolutely no bread. Peaches are nice, though, especially the ones in the heavy syrup. My grandmother used to can her own peaches. She had this big root cellar down under the house, and it was always so cool and damp down there. It smelled like wet dirt and old potatoes. She kept rows and rows of those glass mason jars on these wooden shelves my grandfather built. He was a carpenter, you know. He built half the houses on their street. He had these big, rough hands with calluses all over them from the hammers and the hand saws. You don't see craftsmanship like that much anymore, everything is just put together so fast with nail guns... \n\nI'm sorry, what was I saying? Oh, the words. No, they're just completely gone right out of my head. I can't remember a single one of them.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to this past week, can you tell me about the last time you spoke to someone on the telephone or had a visitor come to your house? What did you talk about?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that. A telephone call or a visitor... I don't really get visitors. The mail carrier comes by, but we don't talk. He just drops the letters in the box and keeps walking. He wears those blue shorts even when it's raining, which always seemed so strange to me. I'd be freezing in shorts in the rain. \n\nBut a telephone call... you know, I don't think I've talked to anyone on the phone this past week. The phone rings sometimes, but it's usually just those recorded messages telling me I've won a cruise or something silly like that. Who goes on a cruise anyway? Being stuck on a boat in the middle of the ocean just sounds terrifying. I saw a movie once about a boat that flipped completely upside down. Everyone was trying to climb up to the bottom of the ship, which was now the top. They had to swim through flooded hallways and climb up elevator shafts. It made me feel so out of breath just watching it. I don't like movies where people are trapped. I much prefer those old musicals, the ones with Gene Kelly where they're tapping and singing in the rain. Now that's a nice way to spend an afternoon, watching people dance. \n\nSo, no, I don't think I've had any phone calls or visitors this week. Just me and the television, really.\n\nDoctor: Earlier you told me you never had any children and have always been on your own, but you also mentioned working at a school office for many years and having a son-in-law who offered to buy you a television. Can you help me understand—do you have family members like a son or daughter, or am I confused about what you said?\n\nPatient: Oh, I... did I say that? About a son-in-law? I suppose I did... I'm sorry, my mind just gets so jumbled sometimes. No, I don't have any children. I've always been on my own. I did work at the school, though, for a very long time. It was a good job, most days. \n\nBut a son-in-law... you know, I think I was thinking about my neighbor, Mrs. Gable. She has a son-in-law who buys her things. He bought her this massive television, one of those flat ones that takes up half the wall. It looks like a movie theater screen right in her living room. I don't know why anyone needs a television that big. The people on the screen are larger than life, it's almost startling when they look right at the camera. I prefer my little set. It's a Zenith, I've had it for years. The picture tube still works perfectly, though sometimes the color gets a little green around the edges if I don't adjust the antenna just right. My father used to fix televisions, back when they had all those vacuum tubes inside. He had this big caddy full of them, all different sizes, and he'd test them one by one... \n\nBut no, no family of my own. Just me. I don't know why I said it was my son-in-law. I must have just gotten confused with Mrs. Gable's stories.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I like a little cream in mine, the real heavy cream, not that powdered stuff that clumps up when you stir it. It looks like little chalk pieces floating around in the mug...\n\nOh, wait. What were those words again? Apple, table, coin. Yes, that's right. I'll remember them.\n\nDoctor: A few minutes ago, I gave you three words to remember. Can you tell me what those three words were?\n\nPatient: Three words... oh dear. I knew you were going to ask me that. I told myself to hold onto them, but... you know, I usually have to write things down. If I don't write down my grocery list on a little scrap of paper, I forget half of what I went in for. \n\nLike just the other day, I went to the market for a loaf of bread and came out with three cans of peaches and absolutely no bread. Peaches are nice, though, especially the ones in the heavy syrup. My grandmother used to can her own peaches. She had this big root cellar down under the house, and it was always so cool and damp down there. It smelled like wet dirt and old potatoes. She kept rows and rows of those glass mason jars on these wooden shelves my grandfather built. He was a carpenter, you know. He built half the houses on their street. He had these big, rough hands with calluses all over them from the hammers and the hand saws. You don't see craftsmanship like that much anymore, everything is just put together so fast with nail guns... \n\nI'm sorry, what was I saying? Oh, the words. No, they're just completely gone right out of my head. I can't remember a single one of them.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to this past week, can you tell me about the last time you spoke to someone on the telephone or had a visitor come to your house? What did you talk about?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that. A telephone call or a visitor... I don't really get visitors. The mail carrier comes by, but we don't talk. He just drops the letters in the box and keeps walking. He wears those blue shorts even when it's raining, which always seemed so strange to me. I'd be freezing in shorts in the rain. \n\nBut a telephone call... you know, I don't think I've talked to anyone on the phone this past week. The phone rings sometimes, but it's usually just those recorded messages telling me I've won a cruise or something silly like that. Who goes on a cruise anyway? Being stuck on a boat in the middle of the ocean just sounds terrifying. I saw a movie once about a boat that flipped completely upside down. Everyone was trying to climb up to the bottom of the ship, which was now the top. They had to swim through flooded hallways and climb up elevator shafts. It made me feel so out of breath just watching it. I don't like movies where people are trapped. I much prefer those old musicals, the ones with Gene Kelly where they're tapping and singing in the rain. Now that's a nice way to spend an afternoon, watching people dance. \n\nSo, no, I don't think I've had any phone calls or visitors this week. Just me and the television, really.\n\nDoctor: Earlier you told me you never had any children and have always been on your own, but you also mentioned working at a school office for many years and having a son-in-law who offered to buy you a television. Can you help me understand—do you have family members like a son or daughter, or am I confused about what you said?\n\nPatient: Oh, I... did I say that? About a son-in-law? I suppose I did... I'm sorry, my mind just gets so jumbled sometimes. No, I don't have any children. I've always been on my own. I did work at the school, though, for a very long time. It was a good job, most days. \n\nBut a son-in-law... you know, I think I was thinking about my neighbor, Mrs. Gable. She has a son-in-law who buys her things. He bought her this massive television, one of those flat ones that takes up half the wall. It looks like a movie theater screen right in her living room. I don't know why anyone needs a television that big. The people on the screen are larger than life, it's almost startling when they look right at the camera. I prefer my little set. It's a Zenith, I've had it for years. The picture tube still works perfectly, though sometimes the color gets a little green around the edges if I don't adjust the antenna just right. My father used to fix televisions, back when they had all those vacuum tubes inside. He had this big caddy full of them, all different sizes, and he'd test them one by one... \n\nBut no, no family of my own. Just me. I don't know why I said it was my son-in-law. I must have just gotten confused with Mrs. Gable's stories.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, it does surprise me a bit when you point it out, yes. I don't always catch it myself. My mind just sort of wanders off, you know? Like a little boat untied from the dock, just drifting out into the water. 

I used to love going to the lake when I was a little girl. We had this small rowboat, painted a bright, cherry red. My uncle painted it himself. He was always painting things—fences, chairs, the side of the barn. He said a fresh coat of paint could make the whole world feel new again. He smelled like turpentine and peppermint. He always kept those little peppermint candies in his shirt pocket, the ones wrapped in the crinkly clear plastic. I'd sit in the boat and he'd row us out to the middle of the lake, and it was so quiet out there. You could just hear the water lapping against the wood. It was so peaceful. The water was this deep, dark green, almost black in some places, and I used to wonder what was swimming around way down at the bottom... 

But, oh, what were you asking? About getting mixed up? Yes, I suppose I don't really notice when I say things that don't match. It just feels right in my head when I'm saying it, until someone tells me otherwise.
